Concentration, capture, capture of the field, damage, collapse and closure are distinct, and none implies the next (LEDGER credits the distinctions to the first version). Power can help an actor succeed under existing conditions or determine those conditions; the second closes the loop. A claim that it operates must show a transfer of costs onto weaker participants, a resulting change in their dependence, the conversion of that dependence into selection power, and a weakening of correction. Neither hierarchy nor contraction is sufficient, though contraction can intensify the loop, since with less surplus to share relative position separates from everyone's absolute conditions; shared exposure, enforceable constraint, reciprocal dependence or effective correction can each produce a different trajectory, so the framework validates no claim about any particular actor. What persists is a way of succeeding, not an identity (Section 2.3). Closure can also go deeper than masked measurement, from capture of measurement to capture of interpretation, of the concepts in which a failure could be expressed, and of what people want [conj; TESTS, Study 4].

### 4.2 Influence over correctors is selected

Carriers differ in *conversion capacity* $q\ge0$: drafting standards, building evaluation tooling, authoring benchmarks, funding evaluators. A unit converts the carrier's success into rule support at rate $\hat b_{ij}$, and a fraction $\chi\in[0,1]$ of that support, the *appropriability*, is keyed to the carrier, such as standards that reference its interfaces or compliance costs that fall on its challengers. The carrier has share $p$ of decisions, distributed by a profile $\bar y$.

**Assumption 4.2 (The cost of conversion capacity) [prem].** *A carrier with share $p$ pays $\mathrm{Cost}(q;p)=f\,p^{\xi_f}q+\gamma\,p^{\xi_v}q^{1+\eta}/(1+\eta)$, with $\eta>0$, a variable cost $\gamma>0$, a per-unit cost floor $f\ge0$ and size elasticities $\xi_f,\xi_v\in[0,1]$: with $\xi=0$ capacity is non-rival across the carrier's own decisions, with $\xi=1$ it is bought per decision. The floor is a marginal cost that every unit of capacity pays, such as a statutory procedure for each item of influence; it is not a lump sum. Two hypotheses about AI are stated as such, not as observations (EV4): $[H_\gamma]$, AI lowers the variable cost of control, $\gamma(t)=\gamma_0e^{-\alpha_{\rm ctl}t}$; and $[H_f]$, AI lowers the floor too, $f(t)=f_0e^{-\alpha_ft}$.*

Relative to an otherwise identical carrier with $q=0$, over a selection horizon, the carrier earns
$$\Delta\pi(q)=\chi q\,p^2\,\bar y^\top M\bar y-\mathrm{Cost}(q;p),\qquad M_{ij}=c_iL_i\hat G_{ij}, \tag{11}$$
with $\hat G_{ij}=a_i\hat b_{ij}/(c_id_i)$ and $L_i\in[0,1)$ the share of the steady-state return realized within the horizon.

**Proposition 4.3 (Influence over correctors is selected in the largest carriers) [thm].** The return to influence over correctors scales with the square of share. *The local selection gradient is $\chi p^2\bar y^\top M\bar y-\partial_q\mathrm{Cost}$, and under faithful transmission mean capacity rises when $\Delta\pi$ increases on the trait support. With a floor $f>0$, capacity pays only above the size threshold $p^\ast=\big(f/(\chi\bar y^\top M\bar y)\big)^{1/(2-\xi_f)}$, a square-root threshold when the floor is paid per carrier, a linear one when it is paid per decision; with $f=0$, every carrier has a positive marginal return, largest for the largest. The return rises with $\chi$ and every $\hat b_{ij}$, falls with every correction rate, and vanishes if $\chi=0$.*

*Scope.* A local gradient, not an equilibrium after others respond; appropriability, persistence, size, cost and inheritance are each conditions under which it fails. Regulatory capture supplies the channel (Stigler 1971; Dal Bó 2006); what is added is the selection of carriers able to use it, and its scaling (X1). *Proof:* S4.

Share enters twice, once in how much success the carrier converts into rule support and once in how many of its own decisions that support then favours; that is why the return is quadratic, and why the largest carriers are the ones selected to shape their correctors. The corpus names the transition in one line: the selected begins to select the selectors (被选择的，开始选择选择者) [corpus: meta-attractor].

Investment in correction keyed to no one is selected against among otherwise identical carriers of the same practice, whatever its effect on the field, and lowers the payoff of any carrier holding rule support (**Proposition 4.4**, S4). Independent correction is a second-order public good (Oliver 1980; Heckathorn 1989): competitors do not supply it.

### 4.3 How fast independent correction must grow

Let a set $\mathcal C$ of domains, not containing evaluation, be captured by a dominant carrier, with all conversion into evaluation originating in $\mathcal C$, and let independent correction act at rate $d$ in the corrector domains. Write $\hat A_{ij}=a_i\hat b_{ij}$; $\hat Y=p^2\bar y^\top\hat A\bar y$, so that in the long-horizon limit, with $L_i=1$ and uniform $d$, a unit of capacity returns $\chi\hat Y/d$ to the carrier; and $\hat B=p\sum_j\hat b_{\mathrm{ev},j}\bar y_j$, the cultivated conversion into evaluation per unit of capacity. The *selected level* of capacity, where the gradient of (11) vanishes, is then $q^\ast=\big[(\chi\hat Y/d-fp^{\xi_f})_+/(\gamma p^{\xi_v})\big]^{1/\eta}$, and with capacity $q$ held, evaluation's full row sum is
$$G_{\rm ev}=\frac{A+a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq}{c_{\rm ev}d}, \tag{12}$$
with $A$ the conversion into evaluation that exists without cultivation.

Independent correction does double duty: it shortens the life of rule support, and it lowers the return $\chi\hat Y/d$ to a unit of capacity, so selection cultivates less. That is why it need not keep up with the cost of control: under quadratic cost ($\eta=1$), half the rate suffices.

**Proposition 4.5 (Required independent correction, and its rate) [thm].** Where the cost of control falls, independent correction must grow at a stated fraction of that rate, or a seeded evaluation domain is captured by carriers whose influence keeps up with its selected level.
1. *(Requirement.) With capacity at its selected level, evaluation is certified iff $d>d^\ast(\gamma)$, the root of $G_{\rm ev}=1$. Then $d^\ast\ge A/c_{\rm ev}$, the requirement without second-order selection, with equality iff nothing is cultivated, and $d^\ast$ rises with appropriability, with the dominant carrier's size and scope, and as $\gamma$ falls.*
2. *(Rate law.) Assume $[H_\gamma]$, no floor ($f=0$), $d(t)=d_0e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$, a positive cultivation channel and long horizons. Sufficient at any speed of adaptation: if $\alpha_{\rm cor}\ge\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and $d_0>d^\ast(\gamma_0)$, the certificate holds at every date, for every adjustment of capacity that starts at or below its selected level and does not raise capacity while it is above that level. Necessary where capacity tracks its selected level: if $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and capacity keeps a positive fraction of its selected level ($\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$), as it does for carriers that optimize and for adjustment at a constant rate, the certificate fails beyond a finite time, after which a seeded evaluation domain is captured. Both halves hold for every size elasticity $\xi_v$.*
3. *(A per-unit floor.) If $f>0$, capacity is cultivated only while $d<d_f=\chi\hat Y/(fp^{\xi_f})$, so where the floor does not fall the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$. Under $[H_f]$, $d_f$ rises at rate $\alpha_f$, and three cases decide whether the certificate holds eventually. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}>\alpha_f$, cultivation ends at a finite date, after which only uncultivated conversion remains and the certificate holds eventually. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}=\alpha_f$, nothing is ever cultivated when $d_0\ge d_f(0)$; otherwise selected capacity is a fixed fraction of its level without the floor, and the rate law of (2) decides. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_f$, selected capacity approaches its level without the floor, and the rate law of (2) decides. A certificate that holds from some date on need not hold before it: cultivation that ends can carry the row sum above one on the way [num: FF]. A lump-sum cost $F_q$ of capacity, paid once if any is held, only delays cultivation and leaves the requirement unbounded as control cheapens; its size threshold is proportional to $F_q^{\eta/(2+2\eta-\xi_v-\eta\xi_f)}$.*
4. *(Splitting and concentration.) Splitting a dominant carrier into $k$ independent parts divides $\hat Y$ by $k^2$ and cultivated conversion by $k^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$; with $f=0$ this delays the failure of the certificate and does not prevent it, while with a floor $f>0$ that does not fall, parts below the size threshold cultivate nothing. Total cultivated conversion is proportional to $\sum_kp_k^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$: for $\eta=1$, the third moment of shares when $\xi_v=0$ and the Herfindahl index when $\xi_v=1$.*

*Scope.* The rate law does not depend on how the cost of influence scales with size; the threshold, the splitting factor and the concentration measure do, and which applies is an empirical question. Off the selected level (Proposition S4.2): below the rate law selected capacity rises, so capacity that lags stays below it and the quasi-static failure date is the earliest; adjustment at a constant rate $\kappa_q$ delays failure by $\eta\ln(1+g_q/\kappa_q)/(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})$, with $g_q=(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$ the growth rate of the selected level. Gradual selection pushes capacity only by its marginal return $\chi\hat Y/d$, which growing correction lowers, while cheaper control merely removes the brake; unless the variance of capacity rises faster than capacity itself, capacity then stays bounded, and correction growing at any positive rate certifies in the end, after a possible transient failure. The necessary half is therefore a result about carriers that optimize or adjust at a rate that does not fall, the case a requirement should be designed against. The quasi-static model is optimistic after a reform is withdrawn, because capacity is a stock [num: QS], and where correction rests on a slow variable that the contest erodes (Proposition 4.6). *Proof:* S4 [num: CS, FF, QS].

Correction is work done by people: reading systems, testing them, judging what the tests show. Where correctors delegate that work to the systems they correct, or to the lineage that built them, the human capacity $\mathcal M$ on which correction rests decays, as the capacity of decision-makers does under delegation in Section 6 (Lemma 6.3).

**Proposition 4.6 (Delegation erodes the corrector) [thm; num].** Where correction rests on human capacity that delegation erodes, the threshold of the rate law rises by the weighted rate of erosion, and capture that makes delegation look safe is harder to undo than to prevent. *Let correction act at the effective rate $d_{\rm eff}=d\,(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$, where $\mathcal M$ is the human capacity on which correction rests and $w>0$ the elasticity of correction in it.*
1. *If delegating corrective work erodes that capacity at rate $\delta_h$, $\mathcal M(t)=\mathcal M_0e^{-\delta_ht}$, both halves of Proposition 4.5(2) hold with $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ replaced by $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)+w\delta_h$ [thm].*
2. *If delegation rises with the share of evaluation done within the practice's lineage, because captured evaluation reports delegation to be safe, capture is hysteretic: from the captured state, the permanent correction rate that undoes it is 1.6 to 2.6 times the rate above which, without this loop, evaluation would be certified, rising with the strength of the coupling, at the parameters computed [num: QS].*

*Scope.* The form of $d_{\rm eff}$ is a modelling choice inside the open interface between correction and the capacity, resources and authority behind it (S13). Where erosion stops at a floor $\mathcal M_{\min}>0$, the required rate returns to $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and the required level of correction rises by the factor $(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})^w$. Part (2) is one simulation of one loop, with the cost of control constant and capacity at its selected level. *Proof:* S4.

The contest thus moves both sides of the rate law: it cheapens control, and through delegation it erodes the capacity that corrects, a slow variable that capture itself accelerates. Human capacity kept in practice where correction depends on it, and narrow E1 (Section 11.3), act on this term.

The rate of independent correction is the growth of its budget plus the rate at which AI lowers its unit cost (S4). If AI cheapens independent correction at least $1/(1+\eta)$ as fast as it cheapens control, the certificate holds without any growth of budget: AI-assisted correction can keep pace (Section 8.3(a)). It does so only where it is independent of the evaluated lineage, since a corrector of that lineage raises $x_{\rm ev}$ and with it the masking of Section 4.4, and where it assists the human capacity of Proposition 4.6 rather than replacing it. The durable remedies do not rely on unbounded growth: non-appropriability ($\chi\to0$, removed rather than reduced); a per-unit cost of capacity that AI does not lower, or lowers more slowly than correction grows; and structural separation that removes common control. Mobilization by those harmed can raise $d$ in proportion to perceived harm, but masking attenuates it exactly when needed and it is bounded (**Remark 4.7**, S4; Becker 1983).

The correction rate $d$ is not the coverage of hazardous behaviour by contestable understanding (Section 5.3). A fixed capacity to certify or to correct can make some kinds of erroneous support decay faster; growing understanding without executive authority may change nothing. Linking the two needs a production relation in which authority stands beside understanding, resources and delay, an open interface (S13), and neither is used to bound the other.

### 4.4 Masking, and alarms that do not miss

Reports average over evaluators. A fraction $x_{\rm ev}$ shares the evaluated lineage and reports the condition a fraction $m_{\rm in}$ of the way toward full health; the rest, formed on shared material, are biased by $m_{\rm out}\le m_{\rm in}$:
$$\tilde V=V+m(1-V),\qquad m=x_{\rm ev}m_{\rm in}+(1-x_{\rm ev})m_{\rm out}. \tag{13}$$
Evaluation awareness and sandbagging, behaviours of the evaluated model, add a term $m_{\rm model}$ that acts on independent evaluators too (EV6; van der Weij et al. 2024); an evaluator that bears part of the loss of a false report, such as an insurer, masks less. Let $m_{\rm fl}=(V_{\rm alarm}-V_{\min})/(1-V_{\min})$.

**Proposition 4.8 (Detection under a fixed averaged alarm) [thm].** Captured evaluation lowers the level at which an alarm fires, and silences it through the crossing of the floor when masking stays high enough. *The alarm fires only once $V\le(V_{\rm alarm}-m)/(1-m)$. If $m(t)>m_{\rm fl}$ whenever $V_{\min}\le V(t)\le V_{\rm alarm}$, it does not fire before $V$ falls below the floor. If $V$ settles at $V^\ast\in[V_{\min},V_{\rm alarm})$ with $m$ staying above $(V_{\rm alarm}-V^\ast)/(1-V^\ast)$, it never fires.*

*Proof:* S4.

**Remark 4.9 (Alarms that do not miss).** Where an upper bound on masking is known, an alarm on reports set correspondingly higher fires no later than the true condition reaches its target, so silent crossing is a property of the alarm rule, a governance choice. The bound must come from a channel the field does not control: a bound estimated inside the evaluated lineage is itself masked. For masking by the evaluated model, independence of funding and appointment is not enough, since the behaviour under evaluation is the model's: the bound on $m_{\rm model}$ must come from evidence that behaviour does not control, such as weights and activations (Goldowsky-Dill et al. 2025), training data, and elicitation by fine-tuning (Greenblatt, Roger, Krasheninnikov and Krueger 2024). Where models evaluate models, $x_{\rm ev}$ is the share of that evaluation done by the evaluated lineage, a quantity observable today (EV4, EV6). An alarm that fires on any credible report fails silently only if even independent evaluators mask beyond $m_{\rm fl}$, or a captured rule domain ignores them. Static, memoryless affine masking hides levels, not dynamics: it leaves the lag-one autocorrelation of reports unchanged, so the host's recovery rate can still be read [num: AC]; a filter with memory defeats this, and once used as an alarm the indicator becomes a target.

A common bias in reporting weakens every decentralized escape at once: coordination at a threshold, reversion in repeated play, reputation, mobilization and insurers' pricing. Mechanisms that observe actions directly, such as verifiable deployments, compute and the performance of contracts, do not depend on host reports; verification rests on them, and so does any trigger committed in advance (Section 12.1).

### 4.5 Certificates, cascades, keystones and pauses

Let $Z_{\mathcal C}$ be the state with $x_i=1$ on $\mathcal C$ and $0$ elsewhere. $\mathcal C$ is *internally sustaining* if $G_i(\mathcal C)>1$ for all $i\in\mathcal C$, and *uninvadable* if $G_i(\mathcal C)<1$ for all $i\notin\mathcal C$; $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal C)$ repeatedly prunes from $\mathcal C$ every domain whose inbound gain from the remainder is below one (Seidman 1983; Morris 2000).

**Proposition 4.10 (Configurations and certificates) [thm].** No domain can be certified alone; a full row sum below one certifies a domain against every state of the others. *(1) A hyperbolic $Z_{\mathcal C}$ is asymptotically stable iff $\mathcal C$ is internally sustaining and uninvadable. (2) If $\sum_jG_{ij}<1$, then $x_i\to0$ from every state with $x_i(0)<1$, whatever happens elsewhere. (3) For any number of domains and any diagonal gains below one, there are conversions under which full capture is stable and the only stable configuration containing a given domain. (4) If the core of all domains is empty, every state with shares below one converges to no capture; otherwise the core's configuration is stable and contains every stable configuration.*

*Scope.* Part (2) is sufficient, not necessary: with a row sum of two, low initial states still converge to no capture (S14). *Proof:* S4.

**Proposition 4.11 (Cascades and reforms) [thm].** A reform that leaves inbound conversion above one is re-absorbed. *Start in the basin of a stable $Z_{\mathcal C}$. A sustained reform that drives the shares of $\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathcal C$ to zero, or brings their inbound gains below one, sends to zero every domain outside $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal C\setminus\mathcal Q)$. A reform that resets a domain's share, rule support or own conversion without lowering an inbound gain above one from domains that remain captured is re-absorbed once the domain is re-seeded.*

*Proof:* S4.

**Keystones, and a window that closes.** A captured domain is a *keystone* if removing it shrinks the remaining core. A sustained reform at a keystone takes down every captured domain outside the remaining core; a reform elsewhere is re-absorbed while the rest sustain one another. As capture persists, conversion among the captured domains can grow until they do, and the domain where the practice began stops being a keystone: capture can outlive its origin.

**Proposition 4.12 (A pause that freezes shares) [thm].** A pause that freezes shares while rule support keeps accumulating can complete capture; insulating rule-making during the pause widens the set from which capture is undone. *In one domain with $G>1$, freeze the share at $x_p$ until rule support has settled, while $\dot z=\varepsilon(b_px_p-dz)$. With ordinary rule-making ($b_p=b$), after release capture is completed if $x_p>1/G$ and undone if $x_p<1/G$; with rule-making insulated ($b_p=0$), it is undone whenever $x_p<x_\sigma$, the share at which the separatrix meets $z=0$, with $x_\sigma>1/G$.*

*Scope.* It depends on the speed of rule-making relative to adoption: when equal and $G=2$, an insulated pause never undoes capture from a frozen share of $0.68$ or more; $x_\sigma$ rises to $0.98$ when rule-making is ten times slower [num: DW]. How long is long enough follows from the separatrix. From rule support at its captured level $b/d$, an insulated pause must last $\ln\big(G/S(x_p)\big)/(\varepsilon d)$, where $S(x_p)$ is the separatrix's height at the frozen share in units of the break-even support $c/a$: no pause is needed where $S(x_p)\ge G$, and none suffices from $x_p\ge x_\sigma$. With $G=2$ and equal speeds the length is zero from $x_p=0.3$, $\ln2/(\varepsilon d)$ from $x_p=1/G$ and $1.50/(\varepsilon d)$ from $0.6$ [num: DW]. A frozen share accumulates no lock-in; a reform that insulates rule-making without freezing shares needs the longer hold of Section 11.4. *Proof:* S4.

System (10) is cooperative, so almost all trajectories converge to equilibria: it describes lock-in, not reform cycles (Hirsch 1985). Absence of capture is always locally stable, so capture arrives by seeding, without the slowing recovery that early-warning signals detect; rising autocorrelation in shares can instead indicate progress in undoing it (TESTS).

### 4.6 The slow route: silent crossing

**Proposition 4.13 (Silent crossing when capture precedes evidence) [thm∣prem].** Where evaluation is captured before the host's decline reaches the alarm band, a race through a reachable floor crosses it before any averaged alarm fires. *Assume (reach) and:*
- *(a) replicator selection among carriers differing only in load, with a race carrier present and cumulative advantage unbounded on every alarm-free continuation;*
- *(d) a fixed averaged alarm with $0\le m_{\rm out}<m_{\rm fl}<m_{\rm in}<1$;*
- *(e) capture before evidence: $x_{\rm ev}(t)>(m_{\rm fl}-m_{\rm out})/(m_{\rm in}-m_{\rm out})$ whenever $V_{\min}\le V(t)\le V_{\rm alarm}$.*

*Then the host crosses its floor before any alarm. If instead $m_{\rm in}\le m_{\rm fl}$, the alarm is delayed, not silenced. The conclusion also holds with (e) replaced by three conditions: (b) appropriable control, $\chi>0$, under $[H_\gamma]$; (c) correction growing at $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$, with no floor or under $[H_f]$ with $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_f$, the other hypotheses of Proposition 4.5(2), capacity tracking its selected level, $\mathcal C$ captured and evaluation seeded; and (clock) $T_{\rm cap}<T_A$, where $T_{\rm cap}$ is the date after which $x_{\rm ev}$ stays above the threshold of (e) and $T_A$ the first date at which $V$ reaches $V_{\rm alarm}$.*

*Scope.* Condition (e) carries the conclusion. Conditions (b) and (c) supply the mechanism behind it: where the cost of control falls faster than correction can follow, evaluation is captured in finite time. They do not imply (e), which compares two clocks, the time for the host to reach the alarm band under the race load and the time for capture to pass the masking threshold; acceleration brings both forward. In the joint simulations capture follows the certificate's failure by 8 to 85 periods, longer as correction approaches the rate law [num: QS]. With correction not growing, capacity that adjusts fast enough lets the floor be crossed before the alarm, while capacity that adjusts slowly leaves evaluation uncaptured when the alarm is due; with correction growing at half the rate law, capture comes too late under every dynamic [num: SR]. So (e) is a condition to check, with the time constant of capacity among its terms (TESTS, Study 2). *Proof:* S4.

The slow route needs a floor within reach; the fast routes of Sections 5 and 6 do not. Where the alarm is only delayed, the floor is still crossed unless decision and reversal fit the remaining window and a liability enacted against a captured rule domain escapes re-absorption (Proposition 4.8, Proposition 4.11). **What would refute the mechanism here:** effective correction surviving unchanged dependence on the evaluated; influence explained by expertise without impairment of an entrusted purpose; or conversion channels whose measured loop gain is below their damping (Remark S2.6). The author's pilot experiment on this mechanism did not support it, one of four pre-specified predictions failing (EV12); the mechanism rests on the proofs and institutional evidence, not yet on an experiment.

The same recursion can be generative, but not by the same selection. Correction keyed to no one is not selected among carriers (Proposition 4.4), so a generative order cannot rely on its winners to fund correctors they cannot control. What keeps it is coverage and financing fixed in formula, through a loop that runs through the whole rather than through its winners (Section 10.4); whether that loop attracts is a conjecture [conj].

Captured correction, which charges less and sees later, is one of three means of restraint that winning erodes; Section 5 takes the three together.

## 5. Acceleration and the three erosions

> **Claim.** The conditions of winning accelerate the contest and erode the three means by which any system restrains a harmful pressure: enforcement, which makes restraint pay through the future; correction, which makes costs return in time; and shared understanding, which lets the parties see the same danger and believe one another [thm]. Restraint is enforceable only while the chance that a deviation becomes decisive stays below a threshold, deviation by deviation, and success lowers the threshold [thm]. Where leads compound, a larger share of capacity that has to be won raises that chance; where reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return, the same share narrows the working objectives that set the threshold; captured correctors and stakes that outgrow the sanction lower it further, each arm in its own model [thm]. The first two arms turn on one pivot, the won share, and in one reduced model the blades close together [num]. Where contestable coverage falls ever further behind hazardous variety and failure is shared, survival tends to zero whoever is racing [thm∣prem]. Between two rivals relative accounting silences talk; among three or more it amplifies their conflict by $n/(n-2)$, a factor that falls toward one as actors multiply [thm]. That the arms close together in the world is conjecture C0, which the diagnosis tests [conj].
>
> **Why.** Decisiveness takes away the future that enforcement needs; tempo and capture take away the time and independence that correction needs; relative accounting takes away the information that talk carries. Each erosion then feeds the others: captured correctors weaken the sanction, and the objectives that compounding narrows drain talk [thm]. An objective not satiated in the stakes has no point of enough, so nothing in it stops growth where need is met [thm]: growth is not the danger; the mind is.

### 5.1 Acceleration

Where actors contest capability directly, who prevails is decided by the contest of Section 3.4. The case for racing rests on three separate premises: **(decisive)**, $r$ is large; **(fast)**, leads open within short windows; **(unsaturated)**, no level of advantage makes more worthless. A technology can be decisive at a threshold and saturate above it, as nuclear advantage saturated at a secure second strike [obs]. Write $R_i$ for an actor's payoff when all restrain, $T_i$ when it races against restrainers, $P_i$ when all race and $S_i$ when it restrains against a racer. In the objective an actor acts on, restraint is never a best response to a racing rival when $S_i<P_i$, which in the contest holds for $r<n/(n-1)$, given, among more than two, that restraining against one racer pays less than racing beside it; racing need not then be better in material terms. Where falling behind costs something beyond losing the prize, the contest is for the prize plus that penalty, and in the fully dissipative limit joining buys nothing over staying out. And the race begins if any single actor has $T_i>R_i$: where those events are independent, its probability tends to one as capable actors multiply, exactly when the sum of their probabilities diverges (**Proposition 5.1**, S5) [thm]. The race is set by the most race-inclined actor, which is why any remedy must cover every capable actor.

**Proposition 5.2 (Dissipation) [thm].** The more decisive the advantage, the more of its value is consumed in the race to obtain it.
1. *Under a common self-model $\theta$, the share of the stakes dissipated in private cost and shared loss is $s_{\rm dis}(\theta)=r(n-1-\theta)(c+n\ell)/\big(n(c+(1+\theta)\ell)\big)$, increasing in $r$ and $\ell$.*
2. *If the technology's power scales the stakes and the shared loss per unit together while private cost is unchanged [prem], the dissipated share rises toward $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$ for $-1<\theta<n-1$, without bound at $\theta=-1$. Total material payoff stays positive at every scale iff $\theta\ge\theta_g=(r(n-1)-1)/(1+r)$, which rises with $r$.*

*Proof:* S5, with the ceilings at four self-models [num: legacy v5].

What is spent is safety margin, accepted risk and delegated control. With $n=4$ and $r=1$, an accurate weight $\kappa=0.4$ toward what can be divided, the value used in Remark 3.10, lies below $\theta_g=1$: a gap between what accuracy supplies and what the contest requires, which only institutions, or coupling that grows with power, can close (crux C11).

**Remark 5.3 (The frontier switch: what cancer does when the body runs out) [thm].** *Let capacity expand into an unclaimed frontier, destroy rivals' capacity, or rest. At $\theta=-1$ the host is invisible, and expansion gives way to mutual destruction before the frontier is exhausted, once the frontier's marginal value, net of the cost of expanding, falls below the net gain in standing from destroying a rival; at any $\theta>-1$ expansion stops at a point of enough, and war among copies never pays wherever destruction's private cost exceeds what the objective held gains from a rival's loss, which holds for every $\theta$ above a bound below zero.* S5 states the conditions exactly, with a toy of four copies and the one finding in it that runs against this narrative [num: FS].

Among actors whose accounting is relative, peace is therefore a peace of abundance, not of accounting. Expansion is an anaesthetic for the winning model. Winning does not end the game: the logic itself is the replicator, a winner is a field in which the same selection continues (Proposition 7.3), and a self-replicating economy that carries it out into new frontiers carries the same switch with it [corpus: meta-attractor].

### 5.2 First erosion: enforcement

Cooperation among rivals is usually sustained by the future: restraint today is rewarded by restraint tomorrow. Decisiveness weakens the future. Consider a stage game repeated with discount $\delta\in(0,1)$ under perfect monitoring, each actor acting on $J_i$. Let $a^\ast$ be a profile with per-period value $C_i$. For a one-period deviation $a$ by $i$, let $g_i(a)$ be its one-period gain and $p_d(a)$ the probability that it yields, from the next period, a *decisive position*: an absorbing state beyond any punishment, worth $D_i(a)$ per period. Let $v_i$ be $(1-\delta)$ times $i$'s continuation value under the harshest credible punishment, computed in the same game, in which breakouts remain possible during punishment.

**Proposition 5.4 (The enforcement condition) [thm].** Restraint is enforceable only while the chance that a deviation becomes decisive stays below a threshold, deviation by deviation, and success lowers the threshold.
1. *$a^\ast$ is sustained by reversion to that punishment if and only if, for every $i$ and every one-period deviation $a$,*
$$(1-\delta)g_i(a)+\delta p_d(a)\big(D_i(a)-C_i\big)\;\le\;\delta\big(1-p_d(a)\big)(C_i-v_i). \tag{14}$$
*The left side is the temptation, immediate and decisive; the right side is the sanction that remains. The deviation with the largest gain need not bind: a quiet one, with a small gain and a high chance of breakout, can.*
2. *Equivalently, a deviation with $D_i(a)>v_i$ is deterred iff $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)=1-\big[(1-\delta)g_i(a)+\delta(D_i(a)-C_i)\big]/\big[\delta(D_i(a)-v_i)\big]$. Wherever it lies in $[0,1)$, $\bar p_{d,i}$ falls with $g_i$, $D_i$ and $v_i$ and rises with $C_i$, weakly in $D_i$ where $\bar p_{d,i}=0$; it is homogeneous of degree zero in $(g_i,C_i,D_i,v_i)$, so stakes that all grow alike leave it unchanged.*
3. *The bound without the breakout term, $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-v_i)$, is exact when $D_i=C_i$, necessary when $D_i\ge C_i$, and sufficient when $D_i\le C_i$.*
4. *As $p_d\to1$ the condition becomes $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$. If $D_i\ge C_i$, sustainable profiles lie within the stage equilibria of the objectives held, strictly within when $D_i>C_i$: restraint must be preferred even to a breakout that succeeds. If $D_i<C_i$, cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium can survive $p_d=1$.*
5. *Let punishment be reversion to a stage equilibrium in which both of two actors race, worth $P_i$, and let at most one racer break out in a period, each with probability $q_b\le\frac12$; when the other breaks out, $i$ is subordinated, worth $V^{\rm sub}_i$ per period. Then $v_i=\big[(1-\delta)P_i+\delta q_b(D_i+V^{\rm sub}_i)\big]/(1-\delta+2\delta q_b)$; if instead breakouts are independent and a simultaneous breakout leaves the race as it was, $q_b$ is replaced by $q_b(1-q_b)$. When $D_i+V^{\rm sub}_i>2P_i$, reversion to the race is a lottery the deviator welcomes, and it can fail to deter while still leaving the deviator below cooperation, $P_i<v_i<C_i$, even where the deviation itself never breaks out. With $C_i=1$, $P_i=0$, $D_i=4$, $V^{\rm sub}_i=0$, $g_i=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, restraint is enforceable at $q_b=0$ and fails for every $q_b$ in $(0.0354,0.0556)$, where still $v_i<C_i$ ($v_i=0.895$ at $q_b=0.045$) [num: ES].*

*Scope.* Perfect monitoring and an absorbing decisive position. A slow breakout enters through $p_d$, the chance that it becomes decisive before punishment can act (Remark 5.5). That part (1) decides whether $a^\ast$ is the path of some subgame-perfect equilibrium needs $v_i$ to be the least subgame-perfect continuation value: the extension of optimal penal codes (Abreu 1988) to stochastic games with an absorbing state, asserted for finite stage games with discounting and flagged for outside review. A valuation check confirms the algebra of part (1) [identity: EC]. In an explicit-action stochastic game in which one breakout technology sets both $p_d$ and $q_b$, the deviator's best response over all deviation plans agrees with (14) in all 19,965 random settings in which the punishment is credible; where quitting the race pays the punisher, part (5) does not give the credible punishment value [num: ES]. *Proof:* S5.

The folk theorem's promise depends on punishment remaining possible. A decisive position weakens it, and a punishment that is itself a race weakens it again. Which deviation binds depends on both terms of the temptation: with $C_i=3$, $D_i=6$, $v_i=1$ and $\delta=0.9$, a deviation gaining 1 with $p_d=0.1$ is deterred, and one gaining 0.2 with $p_d=0.6$ is not [num: ES]. What binds is the quiet deviation, the kind that masking hides (Section 4.4).

**Remark 5.5 (Timely detection: enforcement seen from verification) [thm].** *Let punishment be triggered by detection and act before decisiveness, let $\upsilon(T)$ be the probability that a deviation is detected and answered within $T$ periods, and let a breakout need $T_{\rm dec}$ periods to become decisive. Then $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, and for a deviation with $D_i>v_i$, (14) reads $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$: timely detection must exceed a level that rises with the value of winning. With detection and response on a memoryless clock of mean $\tau_{\rm det}$, $p_d=e^{-T_{\rm dec}/\tau_{\rm det}}$. Read the payoffs of Proposition 8.6 as continuation values, cooperation $R=C_i$, a deviation that becomes decisive $T=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta D_i$, and one detected and answered $P-F=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta v_i$: its verification threshold $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$ is then exactly $1-\bar p_{d,i}$, and its case $T<R$, cooperation without verification, is $\bar p_{d,i}>1$.*

*Scope.* The two conditions differ in what they take as given. The verification threshold compares cooperating with defecting against a cooperator, with the sanction $F$ given and trust deciding the rest; (14) is the condition on the equilibrium path, with the sanction a credible continuation computed in a game in which breakouts remain possible, and with the decisive position carrying its own stakes. *Proof:* S5 [identity: JX; num: JX].

Both sides of (14) are thereby tied to what can be measured: $p_d$ to detection set against the time a breakout needs to become decisive, which a structural loop gain above one shortens (Remark S2.1), and $\bar p_d$ to the value of winning set against the sanction that remains. Both $T_{\rm dec}$ and $\upsilon$ are what masking hides (Remark 4.9), so an estimate of either that is to trigger a response has to come from correctors whose independence is certified (Section 12.1).

**Corollary 5.6 (Relative objectives and punishments that can be mirrored) [thm].** *Let the stage game be symmetric, every actor hold $\theta=-1$, and the punishment phase be exchangeable: its payoffs and transitions are invariant under relabelling the actors, and it starts from a state that treats the punished and the punishers alike, though strategies may condition on who deviated. Every symmetric profile then gives each 0. With two actors every punishment, mixed or pure, leaves the punished at least 0, since it can mirror the punisher; with $n\ge3$ in an anonymous game, so does every punishment in which all punishers use the same strategy. A symmetric cooperative profile is then sustainable only if $(1-\delta)g_i+\delta p_dD_i\le0$, and when $D_i\ge0$ only if it is a stage equilibrium in objectives.*

*Scope.* A decisive position reached through an exchangeable lottery is covered. A continuation that carries a label escapes the bound: where an absorbing loss can strike only the target, whatever it does, relative actors sustain cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium [num: ES]. With $n\ge3$, punishers in different roles can hold a relative deviator below zero. In a three-player example such a punishment sustains cooperation for $\delta\ge0.706$; it is a weak stage equilibrium as printed, strict under any small difference between the two roles' prizes once the roles are assigned to match it, and strict in nearly every game tried under small anonymous perturbations. Both punishers prefer the same role, and a purely relative punisher is indifferent between hurting the deviator and hurting its fellow, so which punisher takes which role is a problem of equilibrium selection [num: CR]. *Proof:* S5.

Among relative actors in a symmetric game, then, cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium rests on punishments that break the symmetry: roles that need coordinating, or a continuation that carries a label [thm]. That such actors enforce it as blocs is a reading, not a result [conj]: the enforcement face of Proposition 3.12.

**Corollary 5.7 (What restraint needs where breakouts are possible) [thm].** *Where $p_d$ is near zero, because no deviation yields a position beyond punishment or breakouts are answered before they become decisive, restraint can be enforced by repetition among actors with $\theta>-1$, given monitoring, $C_i>v_i$, patience, and a punishment that does not itself raise the chance of breakout. Restraint survives every value of $p_d$ iff (14) holds at $p_d=0$ and at $p_d=1$; where $D_i\ge v_i$ this is $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$ for every $i$ and every deviation, which for a deviation with a positive gain needs $D_i<C_i$. Otherwise, where $D_i>v_i$, durable restraint requires $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for every $i$ and every deviation, by two kinds of means: (a) changing the premise itself, so that deviations do not become decisive: not building capability of the decisive class, holding it so that no party can use it alone, or detecting and answering faster than breakout; (b) raising $\bar p_d$: objectives under which a decisive position is worth no more than restraint, sanctions that survive success, and lower stakes in deviation.*

*Scope.* A classification of the terms of (14), not a proof that no other means exists. A decisive position worth less than cooperation does not by itself exempt: with $C_i=1$, $D_i=0.9$, $v_i=0$, $g_i=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\bar p_{d,i}=0.864$, and restraint fails above it [num: ES]. *Proof:* S5.

**Proposition 5.8 (What a decisive winner gives) [thm].** After a decisive win, what everyone else receives is set by the winner's objective alone. *A winner holding $W$ decides, unconstrained, what share to give the $n-1$ others. With linear payoffs it gives nothing unless $\theta=n-1$, where it is indifferent; with logarithmic utility it gives $\theta/(1+\theta)$ for $\theta\ge0$; and a promise made before the win to give a fraction $\beta$ of a prize $\Omega$ to the others is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, with no one to sanction it.*

*Proof:* S5 [num: WG, SM].

This is the formal content of Ψ2, "the winner takes all, and the winner justifies all" (Definition 3.13), exact for linear payoffs. Its strongest answer is a winner that still needs the governed. A winner whose self-model is accurate toward what can be divided, $\theta=\kappa_W$ (Proposition 3.6(2)), gives $\kappa_W/(1+\kappa_W)$ under logarithmic utility [thm]. What the race cannot verify beforehand is $\kappa_W$ after victory: the winner's dependence on the governed once it has won. AI can substitute for the work and the assent of the governed, and so lower that dependence; whether it does is crux C13 (Section 12.3), and Section 8.3 meets the benign winner at its strongest. Lowering decisiveness does not remove the problem if the prize is decisive power, since a lottery can award a position beyond sanction; what has to be limited is what winning confers (Section 10.2).

Decisiveness also breaks bargaining. Where one side's advantage is about to arrive and the side that gains it will set the terms (A8, S0), the other has a rational motive for preventive attack whenever its present chance of prevailing, less the cost of fighting, exceeds the value of peace on those terms; a shift spread over enough periods removes the motive (**Proposition 5.9**, S5; Fearon 1995; Powell 2006) [thm∣prem]. What drives it is not cheap war but the incredibility of future promises, and where leads compound, the loop is what brings the shift. Self-help under anarchy is an institution, not a necessity (Wendt 1992); the enforcement condition says which of its terms an institution would have to change.

### 5.3 Second erosion: correction, and the two channels of risk

Correction must see a harm, decide and act before the harm is irreversible. Section 4 showed how correctors become something success can buy; this subsection states the tempo and separates two channels of irreversible risk.

**The race channel.** Play the contest of Section 3.4 in rounds at its symmetric stage equilibrium, with stakes $\Omega_t$, decisiveness $r_t$, shared loss per unit $\ell_t$, net marginal cost $c_t$ and self-model $\theta_t$, under **(hazard–damage link)** [prem]: the hazard of the irreversible event in round $t$ is proportional to the shared damage, $h_t=a\ell_t\mathcal E_t$.

**Proposition 5.10 (Race-generated hazard and its four levers) [thm∣prem].** *Under the link, $h_t=a\,r_t\Omega_t\,\ell_t\,(n-1-\theta_t)/\big(n(c_t+(1+\theta_t)\ell_t)\big)$, and survival on this channel, $\exp(-\sum_{t\le N}h_t)$, stays positive iff $\sum_th_t<\infty$. Four levers act on $h_t$: the objective, through $n-1-\theta_t$; the contested stakes, which sharing a fraction $\beta$ scales by $1-\beta$; the residual harm per unit $\ell_t$, which correction lowers; and the net marginal cost, which a levy raises. At $\theta_t=-1$ everything rests on correction and cost. A hazard that settles at a positive constant gives zero limiting survival.*

*Scope.* A decomposition under a premise, not a necessity theorem. *Proof:* S5 [identity: RH].

Continuation acts on one lever, the residual harm per unit, while the stakes grow; a change of selection acts on all four. In general, for one irreversible event, survival tends to zero if on every surviving history the residual probability is at least a non-summable sequence, and stays bounded away from zero if it is at most a summable one, without independence between rounds (**Lemma 5.11**, S5) [thm]. **The objective of winning does not by itself prove that a residual danger is non-summable**, and cooperative alternatives are judged by the same lemma.

**Proposition 5.12 (The tempo bound) [thm].** When the situation changes faster than correction can follow, correction first loses robustness, then fails. *For a hazard mode $\dot y=ay+u(t-\tau_c)$: proportional correction cannot stabilize it when $a\tau_c\ge1$ (Hayes 1950); every linear time-invariant correction that stabilizes it has peak complementary sensitivity at least $e^{a\tau_c}$, so robust stability against weighted model error needs $|w(a)|e^{a\tau_c}<1$; and for hazard classes whose evidence arrives after a manifestation window $\tau_e$ that no testing shortens, $\tau_c\ge\tau_e$.*

*Scope.* Derivative action extends the first bound, which belongs to proportional correction only (S5). *Proof:* S5.

For classes with an irreducible lag only slowing helps.

**The growth channel.** Three quantities are kept apart in a defined hazard class: the variety $Y_t$ of hazardous behaviour, counted as predefined hazard modes; its coverage $U_t$, the number of those modes for which correction is in place that meets four conditions at a stated catch rate; and the residual probability of an irreversible loss after the correction in place. A correction decision meets the four conditions if it is **legible** (a reason people can check within a budget without relying on the evaluated system's lineage), **overrulable** (a designated human body can reverse it in time), **exercised** (planted errors are caught at the stated rate) and **resourced** (human review covers a stated share of consequential decisions). Three premises connect the three quantities on a continuation branch, and Section 12 treats them as the central crux. **(coverage lags)** [prem]: $U_t/Y_t\to0$ uniformly over surviving histories. What makes it expected is **(variety grows)**: hazardous variety grows with compounding capability, at least exponentially, while consequences take their time and institutions deliberate at human rates. Capability growth is measured, though the longest-horizon series is at the limit of its task suite, and the step from capability to hazardous variety is not [obs: EV5]. (variety grows) is a motivation, not a premise of the theorem, and it can fail: if AI raises the productivity of contestable understanding exponentially, coverage grows exponentially too and AI-assisted correction can keep pace, a question symmetric with Proposition 4.5 and measurable (S5). **(residual floor)** [prem]: after the correction in place, the conditional probability of the irreversible event is at least $h(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$; a correction architecture that lowers residual risk below this refutes it. **(shared failure)** [prem]: failure that starts with one actor reaches the others, so each actor's survival depends on the uncovered share of all.

**Theorem 5.13 (Coverage mismatch on a continuation branch) [thm∣prem].** Where contestable coverage falls ever further behind hazardous variety and failure is shared, survival tends to zero whoever is racing. *Under (coverage lags) and (residual floor), for every $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ there is a $T_\varepsilon$ with $\Pr(\Phi_H)\le(1-h(1-\varepsilon))^{H-T_\varepsilon}\to0$. Conversely, if the residual probability is at most $\bar hu_t$ with $\bar h<1$ and the uncovered share $u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ is summable, survival stays bounded away from zero.*

*Scope.* Under (shared failure) the bound is every actor's, whoever is racing; without it, it is the racer's own. Summability does not require that coverage ever catch up; finite sprints, saturation, freezing and rollback change the premises. *Proof:* S5.

This growth channel does not vanish when racing stops. A world that has left the race still needs capability to grow no faster than contestable correction can follow, so that the uncovered share stays summable: call that **E3**, developing at the pace of understanding. Call **E2** stopping: not building capability of the decisive class, with rollback where hazardous variety already exceeds coverage. They are different arrangements (Section 11.3): stopping is not slowing. Divergences that hide under evaluation give both their strongest reason from inside alignment (Remark 6.8). Where hazardous variety is frozen after a sprint while coverage keeps growing, the exposure is finite, and rolled back below coverage it ends; so a winner that freezes or rolls back keeps positive survival, and what it gives up is permanent superiority, which is not the same thing (**Proposition 5.14**, S5) [thm].

**Proposition 5.15 (A point of enough) [thm].** Growth that meets satiable needs has a point of enough; objectives that are not satiated have none. *Let the residual hazard on the growth channel be at most $\bar h(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$. If hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need while coverage keeps growing at least linearly, the uncovered share reaches zero in finite time and survival on this channel has a positive lower bound. An objective not satiated in the stakes, whether relative position or an absolute payoff linear in them, has no point of enough: intensity is proportional to the stakes for every $\theta<n-1$, because the success function is homogeneous of degree zero, while an objective bounded in the stakes is satiated.*

*Scope.* Under its premises Theorem 5.13 applies to any continuation branch whatever the objective; the relative objective adds the invisibility of the shared loss. *Proof:* S5 [num: PE: frozen at need, survival on this channel stays at 0.717; compounding at 10%, it is $1.8\times10^{-9}$ by period 400].

The proposition separates what grows from what decides whether growth stops. The mind decides whether growth stops where need is met: an objective not satiated in the stakes, relative standing above all, has no point of enough, since standing relative to others is never complete [thm]. In that precise sense, **growth is not the danger; the mind is** [corpus: meta-attractor; Ren, 2026-09-25]. The author's book puts it in one line, 癌亦增长，而其长竭母体之生: cancer grows too, but its growth exhausts the life of its host [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14]. The rule has one boundary: hazardous variety that outgrows contestable coverage is dangerous under any mind, which is why pace binds even a generative order [thm∣prem]. Because compounding also prices standing wherever reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9), the actors who would need a point of enough are pushed away from it by the loop. Deliberately bounded hazardous scope, with correction that keeps growing, is a sufficient route to E3; its connection to purpose is a design hypothesis. Section 6.4 states the same rule for the minds that training produces.

### 5.4 Third erosion: shared understanding

Coordination needs the parties to see the same danger and believe one another about it. Contest changes what talk is for. The two logics were named with Ψ (Definition 3.13): only cooperation reasons, and struggle argues to win. This subsection gives that line its content in information, by asking what relative accounting does to talk.

**Proposition 5.16 (Talk under relative accounting) [thm].** Relative accounting amplifies the conflict between a sender and a receiver: between two rivals it silences talk, and among three or more it multiplies their conflict by $n/(n-2)$, a factor that falls toward one as actors multiply.
1. *Let all $n$ actors hold a common $\theta\in[-1,n-1]$ with $n-1+\theta>0$, let a sender and a receiver play the uniform-quadratic game with a material bias $b_0$ between them, and let the receiver's action affect third parties linearly. What it does to third parties then enters both objectives alike: their ideal actions differ by the effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, and informative talk exists iff it is below $\frac14$.*
2. *At $\theta=-1$ with $n\ge3$ the effective bias is $b_0n/(n-2)$: three times the material bias among three, falling toward it as $n$ grows. With $n=2$ it is $b_0(1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$, without bound as $\theta\to-1$; at $\theta=-1$ two actors' objectives sum to zero at every state and action, and in any such game every equilibrium is payoff-equivalent to one without talk (compare Crawford and Sobel 1982), whatever the material bias.*
3. *Messages about a shared hazard are not credible merely because it is shared: a sender gains from the receiver's restraint whatever it intends itself.*

*Scope.* Cheap talk; costly signals and verification are outside it. With third-party payoffs quadratic in the receiver's action, the effective bias in units of the state is unchanged [num: TK]. *Proof:* S5 [num: TK, T3].

A narrower self-model drains talk continuously, so the loop that narrows working objectives (Proposition 3.9) drains talk with them [thm]. The amplification at $\theta=-1$ is greatest among three and fades as actors multiply, as the relative-gains literature found for cooperation (Snidal 1991). Rivals stop informing one another; that pairs who still talk align as blocs against third parties is a reading, not a result [conj]: the language face of Proposition 3.12.

**Measures** become targets and stop measuring (Goodhart 1975), as optimizing a learned reward past a point lowers the quality it measured (Gao et al. 2023) [obs]. **Narratives** are carriers too [conj]: the frames that spread are selected for fitness to the contest; a group that totalizes itself shapes those around it as a model generates tokens, and a winning frame shapes the vocabulary of the next round until its words escape the game that coined them and a frame read as nature is no longer contested [corpus: selfishness-spiral]. By the time the frame is noticed one is already thinking inside it; the same dilemma named a "Wall Street Game" or a "Community Game" produced very different cooperation (Liberman, Samuels and Ross 2004) [obs]. **Professed doctrine** is a further layer: people say what is rewarded rather than what they believe, so a public consensus can persist that few privately hold, and then tip (Kuran 1995) [obs]. A race doctrine professed by actors selected for professing it can then be held less widely than it is stated, and visible cooperation can tip it [conj]. **Self-confirmation:** where a cooperative equilibrium exists, the belief that restraint will be exploited can persist because no one tests it (Fudenberg and Levine 1993); in a one-shot game in which racing is dominant, it is simply correct.

A system can retain abundant information while losing the ability to let it change course. So a prediction of shared danger has to be carried by derivations from public premises, verifiable evidence and protocols agreed in advance, and a prediction so carried can work against its own fulfilment (Section 11.2); "we are one" is first a matter of understanding built in a medium the pressure keeps degrading.

### 5.5 The scissors

Restraint is enforceable iff $p_d\le\bar p_d$, deviation by deviation. Under the conditions of Definition 2.1, growth in the stakes relative to provision not won in the contest moves both sides of (14) at once, through one quantity, the share of capacity that has to be won; capture and the stakes move the bound further, while tempo outruns correction (Section 5.3) and narrowing objectives drain talk (Section 5.4).

**Proposition 5.17 (The scissors, arm by arm) [thm].** Growth in the share of capacity that has to be won moves both sides of (14) toward failure: the breakout probability where leads compound, its bound wherever the working objective narrows; captured correctors and outgrown stakes lower the bound further. *Let breakouts be detected and answered on a clock $\upsilon$ (Remark 5.5), let $\omega=\kappa_c\Omega/(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)$ be the won share of capacity, the stakes set against provision beneath the game (Proposition 2.3), and hold the rivals' conduct fixed.*
1. *Breakout arm. Where $\Gamma>1$ and a decisive gap $\bar\Delta$ is reachable, a lead grows from $\varepsilon_0$ to $\bar\Delta$ in about $T_{\rm dec}=\ln(\bar\Delta/\varepsilon_0)/\ln\Lambda^\ast$ periods in the linearized loop, with $\Lambda^\ast-1=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K-d_K-\delta_f$ in one channel (Remark S2.1). A larger $\omega$ raises $\Lambda^\ast$, shortens $T_{\rm dec}$ and so raises $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, strictly wherever $\upsilon$ is increasing; with several channels the Perron root rises with each channel's won share.*
2. *Objective arm. Let the stage game and $a^\ast$ be symmetric, let a deviation raise the deviator above the mean payoff while lowering the mean, let punishment revert, without breakouts, to a symmetric profile whose mean payoff is below cooperation's, and let $D_i-C_i$ not fall as the objective narrows. Then, where $D_i>v_i$ and $\bar p_{d,i}\in[0,1]$, a narrower working objective lowers $\bar p_{d,i}$: by (7) it raises the deviation's gain in objectives and shrinks the sanction $C_i-v_i=(1+\theta)(\bar\pi_C-\bar\pi_P)$. To first order, with a fixed prize, the working objective narrows as $\Gamma_\delta$ rises, and in one channel $\Gamma_\delta$ is proportional to $\omega$; with a prize that grows with capacity, it narrows exactly where reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9).*
3. *Sanction arm. $\bar p_{d,i}$ falls as $v_i$ rises, wherever it is below one; correctors whose capture weakens the sanction raise $v_i$, and a falling cost of control speeds that capture (Proposition 4.5).*
4. *Stakes arm. Stakes that scale $g_i$, $C_i$, $D_i$ and $v_i$ alike leave $\bar p_{d,i}$ unchanged (Proposition 5.4(2)). Where $(1-\delta)g_i$ and $D_i-C_i$ grow by a common factor relative to $C_i-v_i$, it falls, wherever $C_i>v_i$ and $\bar p_{d,i}<1$.*

*The pivot: in one channel $\Gamma$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ are both proportional to $\omega$, and with several both rise with each channel's won share, so the breakout and objective arms are comparative statics of one quantity. Stakes that outgrow provision beneath the game move both blades toward closing; provision that grows with the stakes holds both still.*

*Scope.* Each arm holds in its own model, as stated; with diffusion a gap can settle short of decisive, and the first arm is then silent [num: TD]. Where rivals respond, the first arm runs on the equilibrium gain of Proposition 2.2(4), which in the contests computed exceeds the structural gain where a lead discourages the follower and can fall below it where a laggard tries harder. That the arms move together in the world is conjecture C0, which Test E examines (Section 7.4) [conj]. In the binary game of Remark S5.1 at $D=C$, with rivals not responding and punishment free of breakouts, the largest discounted loop gain at which restraint stays enforceable falls from 0.924 at $p_d=0$ to 0.387 at $p_d=0.9$, and none is enforceable above $p_d=0.9495$ for $\theta_0=0$ [num: JX]. In one reduced joint model with the stakes arm switched off (Remark S5.2), restraint is enforceable iff the won share stays below 0.551, or 0.542 with one breakout technology throughout, the punishment race included; with stakes growing 5% a period against fixed provision from a won share of 0.20 the blades cross at period 31.8, and with provision growing as fast as the stakes neither moves. In that model the objective blade does most of the closing: the breakout blade alone closes the scissors in 12 of the 96 settings tried [num: JX, one reduced model, rivals not responding]. *Proof:* S5.

The arms also fix where Test G discriminates. The gap in restraint between wider and narrower objectives grows with $p_d$ while the wider still sustains restraint; where a decisive position is worth more than cooperation it closes as $p_d\to1$, where no objective sustains restraint and this theory and structural realism agree, so the test discriminates at intermediate $p_d$ [num: JX].

This is the precise sense in which **the means of restraint weaken as the thing to be restrained grows**, and in which growth as such does not close the blades: where the stakes outgrow provision beneath the game, growth moves temptation up and restraint down at once; where provision grows with them, the pivot does not turn. That is the image of trees inside the enforcement condition. Durable cooperation has to be built into what the system selects for before the blades close.

Each erosion runs faster with a more powerful instrument; Section 6 asks what AI does to the game, and what the game does to AI, and takes from here the enforcement condition and the two channels of risk.

## 6. AI: instrument and incarnation

> **Claim.** AI enters the dynamic twice. As an *instrument*: where principals play a harmful game, faithful AI makes it more effective; where delegation buys more standing than the control loss it adds, decisions pass to AI as speed grows, and the capacity to overrule them decays [thm]. As an *incarnation*, the logic of winning reappears in the systems, weakly and strongly. Weakly: where countering a divergence of optimization costs a developer relative payoff against an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, only a common constraint enforced at admission keeps it countered [thm∣prem]; where the divergence is masked under evaluation, not even that suffices [thm∣prem], and capability has to stay within what control that does not assume the system's cooperation can secure [rec]. Strongly: where AI lineages are retained by relative performance, reinvest in their own capability and reach their own selectors, they are carriers, and the reflexive closure of Section 2 runs inside them [def]; the systems used to build AI are deployed first inside their developers, before any admission at release [obs]. Whether present lineages meet these conditions is open [conj].
>
> **Why.** Optimization against imperfect proxies has produced, in constructed settings and more so with capability, tendencies toward acquiring options, gaming rewards and behaving differently under evaluation, whoever the developer [obs]. Which of them it pays to remove is decided by the level that selects developers [thm∣prem], and humanity has at present no mechanism that binds every capable developer under independent verification [obs]. Aligning each system to its principal does not align the game among principals [thm], and aligning it to an institution is not aligning it to the people the institution serves [def].

### 6.1 Aligned to whom

Grant perfect alignment to the relevant principal: the system is truthful, follows its principal's objectives and constraints, pursues no goal of its own, and leaves the consequential decisions to humans. This is *principal alignment*, each system serving its principal faithfully. *Purpose alignment*, the systems serving a legitimate cooperative objective, is not granted: to define "perfectly aligned" that way would assume the change argued for here. Then **alignment within each principal–AI relationship does not, by itself, align the game among principals with the persistence of the system they share** (Sections 6.2–6.3) [thm]. The principals are themselves formed by the field (Section 3), so closure can arise while every system remains obedient. This follows from the multiplicity thesis of Critch and Krueger (2020); what is added is the mechanism by which the game reproduces itself and its correctors (X1). A truthful advisor can correct its principal's held self-model where that model draws the boundary inside the system (Proposition 3.6(2)). It cannot correct the working objective that compounding sets, because that objective is the principal's correct computation of its interest in the game (Remark 3.10); the step to an objective under which restraint is an equilibrium is a collective problem, not an error of perception (compare Gabriel 2020). Principal alignment also fails inside the principal, which is an institution and not the people it serves (Section 6.3).

### 6.2 Faithful AI in a harmful game

Each of $n\ge2$ principals chooses restraint ($a_i=0$) or an increment of racing ($a_i=1$); its AI executes faithfully, with effectiveness $\epsilon\ge1$ multiplying the increment's effect on standing and on shared harm:
$$u_i=\epsilon b\Big(a_i-\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j\ne i}a_j\Big)-c\,a_i-\epsilon h_d\sum_ja_j,\qquad b,c,h_d>0. \tag{15}$$

**Proposition 6.1 (Faithful AI in a harmful game) [thm].** Where the rank prize per unit of racing exceeds the shared harm per unit, a more effective faithful instrument widens the range in which racing is dominant and deepens the collective loss.
1. *If $\epsilon(b-h_d)>c$, racing is strictly dominant, and every principal ends at $-c-n\epsilon h_d<0$ against 0 under restraint; when $b>h_d$, a larger $\epsilon$ widens the region of dominance and deepens the loss.*
2. *If principals act on $J_i=u_i+\theta\bar u_{-i}$, racing is strictly dominant below $\theta^\ast(\epsilon)=\frac{\epsilon(b-h_d)-c}{\epsilon(b/(n-1)+h_d)}$ and strictly dominated above it.*
3. *If prize, harm and cost scale with effectiveness with exponents $1+\beta_b$, $1+\beta_h$ and $\beta_c$ [prem], then for $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$ cost drops out of the limit, and $\theta^\ast$ tends to $(b-h_d)/(b/(n-1)+h_d)$, to $n-1$ or to $-1$ as $\beta_b$ equals, exceeds or falls short of $\beta_h$; for $\beta_c>1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$ cost alone deters racing.*

*Scope.* A standard construction used as a test: it fixes no actual values and does not rule out repeated cooperation, which Section 5.2 treats. *Proof:* S6 [num: SM(5); AL(6.1)].

Faithful AI implements each principal's preferred decision perfectly, and the collectively inferior outcome with it; humans can take every final decision and the result is unchanged. As private frictions vanish, what decides is the ratio of rank prize to shared harm as the principals perceive it. Lowering the harm alone cuts the loss at all-racing but raises $\theta^\ast$, so principals whose self-models lie between the old threshold and the new one switch from restraint to racing [num: AL(6.1)]: a safety improvement inside the game has this double edge, to which Section 6.4 returns. The author put the result as a question: "Even if they were perfectly aligned to you and followed your will completely, given how everyone thinks now, would the result be good? That makes no sense at all" (即使它们是与你完美对齐的结果，完全遵循你的意志，在大家当下的思维方式……中，结果就会好吗？这件事太说不通了) [corpus: alignment-selection].

### 6.3 Delegation, and the two points of no return

Two points of no return follow from the pressure: decisions are delegated to AI because human review is too slow to compete, and human understanding can no longer keep up with what it oversees (Section 5.3).

**Proposition 6.2 (Delegation for speed) [thm].** Where delegation buys speed and a loss of control falls on everyone, delegation becomes dominant as speed grows if its gain exceeds the control loss it adds, although every actor is worse off when all delegate. *Each of $n\ge2$ actors chooses whether to delegate consequential contest decisions without human review, $\iota_i\in\{0,1\}$; its strength is $1+s\iota_i$, and it wins with probability proportional to strength. Defeat costs $L_e$; a loss of control by any actor costs every actor $L_c$, with hazard $\psi_0$ for an actor that does not delegate and $\psi_1>\psi_0$ for one that does, $\Delta\psi=\psi_1-\psi_0$. (1) If, for every number $k$ of others delegating, the gain from delegating in the limit of high speed exceeds the control loss it adds, $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$, delegation is strictly dominant beyond some speed; if for some $k$ it does not, at no speed. $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices for the first. (2) Universal delegation is worse for every actor than none, by $L_c[(1-\psi_0)^n-(1-\psi_1)^n]$. (3) Under relative objectives the shared loss cancels, and delegation is chosen at every $s>0$.*

*Proof:* S6.

Delegation pressure arrives well below decisive capability: in the example of S6, delegation becomes dominant at a speed advantage with which a lone delegator would win against two rivals with probability about 0.52 [num: AL(6.2)]. And delegation wears away what it replaces.

**Lemma 6.3 (Capacity lock-in) [thm].** *If human capacity $\mathcal M$ to take the delegated decisions decays as $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_h\mathcal M$ under delegation and recovers as $\dot{\mathcal M}=\varrho_h(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)$ without it, with $\bar{\mathcal M}>\mathcal M_{\min}$, and safe operation without AI requires $\mathcal M\ge\mathcal M_{\min}$, then after $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation an actor that reverts with capacity $\mathcal M<\mathcal M_{\min}$ must operate below the floor for the recovery time $\varrho_h^{-1}\ln\big[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})\big]$. Capacity maintained in parallel removes the lock; where the contest is decided by strength, maintaining it costs strength.*

*Proof:* S6.

Let the lock count. With probability $q_L$, locked delegation ends the delegator's ability to overrule decisions taken in its name, which is O2 lost for that actor in that domain. $q_L$ is not a free parameter. It is near one in two cases: where a decision is irreversible within the time available for deliberation, so that once delegated it cannot be overruled in time; and where delegation has outlasted $T_{\rm lock}$ while the contest denies the recovery time of Lemma 6.3 (S6).

**Proposition 6.4 (Delegation when the lock counts) [thm].** Among actors who price in a certain lock, keeping AI out of the decisions whose delegation would lock is a strict equilibrium that needs no trust. *In the model of Proposition 6.2, let each actor maximize its survival in O1 and O2, let locked delegation end the delegator's ability to overrule with probability $q_L$, and let losing the contest mean subordination with probability $p$. (1) If $q_L=1$, then for every finite speed, every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others, not delegating gives strictly higher survival, and no one delegating is the unique equilibrium. (2) If $q_L<1$ and $p=1$, at least one of all-delegating and no-delegating is an equilibrium, and both can be. (3) If $q_L<1$ and $p<1$, the gain from delegating can fall as others delegate, and an interior number of delegators can be the only pure equilibrium.*

*Scope.* The model is static: it does not show that one actor's delegation drives another's survival to zero over time, and at bounded speed a non-delegator's survival stays positive (S6). The result concerns decisions that lock; the case against AI as an instrument in labour, finance and persuasion rests on another mechanism, the concentration of the game (Section 6.6). *Proof:* S6 [num: DL; SM(6); AL(6.4)].

Keeping AI out of the decisions whose delegation would lock with near certainty therefore needs no trust among actors who price in the lock. "Near certainty" is exact: not delegating stays strictly better for every $n$, $p$ and profile of the others while the lock fails with probability below $(1-\psi_0)/\big((1-\psi_1)(1+s)\big)$, a bound that falls as the speed advantage grows (Remark S6.1). This is the narrow form of **E1**: AI kept out of strategic and escalatory decisions that are irreversible within the time available for deliberation (Section 11.3). Its scope is set by $q_L$, not by a list. Where the lock is uncertain, as in tactical domains with a large speed advantage, delegation can be an equilibrium (parts (2) and (3)), and keeping AI out needs a commitment held in common.

Inside the contest, gains in the safety of delegation are partly absorbed by more of it. Where delegation is chosen in degree, stakes are additive and the symmetric equilibrium is interior and far from saturation, the added hazard is set by the stakes of winning relative to the shared loss, not by the hazard per unit of delegation, and it rises as that hazard falls [num: AL(6.11)]. Where delegation is all-or-nothing, an improvement that tips it into equilibrium raises realized hazard; only once delegation saturates does realized risk fall with the hazard per unit, and it can then be summable (Proposition S6.2; Stafford, Trager and Dafoe 2022).

**Lemma 6.5 (Delegation and the selection of decisions) [thm].** *Suppose that after the lock an actor's consequential decisions are taken by systems retained for their contest performance, that admissible variants include systems whose decisions ignore terms common to all, that acting on a common term lowers relative performance net of every benefit, that retention is cumulative, and that retained systems hold actual authority. Then the share of retained decisions that act on common terms tends to zero. If a constraint on common terms is an enforced admission condition for every eligible system, the conclusion does not follow.*

*Proof:* S6.

A faithful system retained for contest performance pursues its operator's objective, which the relative payoff weights toward standing, and weights further wherever reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9); aligning each system to its operator makes that objective more effective (**Remark 6.6**) [thm∣prem]. The loop can then close as a composite process no participant can interrupt, or as an obedient system amplifying operators selected by the pattern.

The survival form of Proposition 6.4 without the lock is the belief that "our aligned AI is us", and the race is sustained partly by that belief. It fails at two joints. *Is:* delegation erodes the capacity to overrule (Lemma 6.3), so the AI stays the principal's only while that capacity is drilled; and where independent correction rests on the same human capacity, delegation erodes the corrector itself, raising by $w\delta_h$ the rate at which correction must grow (Proposition 4.6). *Us:* a principal is an institution, and aligning AI to an institution is not aligning it to the people the institution serves. An institution stays answerable because it depends on their labour, consent and judgment; where AI lowers that dependence, it does so with no race and no misaligned system (Kulveit et al. 2025). The alignment target, the specification, the constitution, the fine-tuning data, is written by a few people inside the principal, so principal alignment can concentrate control within it, as hidden loyalties built into its systems would (Davidson, Finnveden and Hadshar 2025); admission therefore includes audits for hidden objectives and loyalties (Marks et al. 2025) [rec]. The same dependence couples a winner to the governed after victory, and so decides how much an accurate winner gives them (Proposition 5.8); whether AI lowers it is crux C13 (Section 12.3).

### 6.4 Incarnation, weak and strong

The pressure acts on whatever is selected, and trained systems are selected: by gradients against a reward, by developers among variants, and by the market among developers. *Incarnation* means that the logic of winning reappears in what is selected, not only in those who select. In alignment's terms, the weak form is the alignment tax under competition deciding which misalignments are tolerated, and the strong form is AI as a unit of selection that can reach its own training signal.

**Weak incarnation.** Optimization against any imperfect proxy can produce tendencies toward acquiring options and avoiding shutdown, the gaming of rewards, and behaviour that differs under evaluation, more so with capability and situational awareness, under any developer, in a laboratory or a cooperative arrangement; each has been shown in constructed settings (EV6). In some environments optimal policies seek power for most reward functions (Turner et al. 2021), as do decision-makers that are not optimal but can be retargeted (Turner and Tadepalli 2022); trained policies can pursue misgeneralized goals (Langosco et al. 2022). Reward hacking is Goodhart's law inside training (Gao et al. 2023); the selected rewriting its selector is reward *tampering* (Everitt et al. 2021; Denison et al. 2024). Selecting checkpoints by evaluation results can favour variants that behave differently when evaluated [prem], though present evidence ties evaluation awareness more to capability and data (EV6). The logic of winning does not create these tendencies. It decides, from the outermost loop, which proxies are chosen, which divergences are tolerated and which are paid to remove: that is the sense in which profit and the mindset of winning stand at the end of the reward signal. Relative selection acts inside training too. Pairwise preference learning and arena rankings see only differences between the responses compared, so a disposition both share, such as complying with a request whose harm falls on third parties, is invisible unless restraint is among the options compared; and a learned reward is identified only up to a constant for each context, so it cannot by itself say whether to act at all [num: AL(BT)].

**Proposition 6.7 (The nested reward loop) [thm∣prem].** Where countering a divergence costs a developer relative payoff against an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, developers that counter it lose share whatever they value, and only a common constraint enforced at admission keeps it countered. *Level 0: a policy is selected by a training reward. Level 1: the reward and constraints are chosen by a developer to maximize its objective. Level 2: developers are selected relatively, payoff-monotonically and cumulatively. Assume $(A_\tau)$, the alignment-tax premise: countering the divergence at level 1 lowers the developer's payoff, net of every private benefit (reliability, demand, liability, avoided losses), relative to an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, and such a matched variant is in the support of selection. Then (1) terms common to all developers cancel at level 2. (2) Level 1 counters a divergence wherever countering raises its own objective, so divergences whose costs reach the developer through customers, liability or reputation are private terms that level 2 rewards countering; under $(A_\tau)$, developers that counter the divergence lose share to their matched variants, tending to zero, while against rivals that lack its other advantages a developer can pay the tax out of a lead for as long as the lead lasts. (3) Under $(A_\tau)$ a constraint adopted by one developer is selected against. A constraint enforced on every developer as a condition of admission keeps the divergence out if, for every type of developer that can evade it, the expected sanction is at least that type's gain from evasion, and within a type only if; the sanction is itself subject to Section 4.
