Equal incidence also keeps the constraint neutral among those who comply; unequal incidence re-sorts them without letting the divergence back in.*

*Scope.* It does not apply where developers are selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate; a national laboratory that only moves relative selection to the level of states leaves it in force at that level. Where the tax is negative the safeguard spreads without coordination; where it is zero the safeguard is neutral. *Proof:* S6 [num: CI; AL(6.7)].

So the divergences that persist in the innermost optimizer are those whose costs fall on common terms and are not covered by an enforced common constraint. Three classes differ in what removes them. *Privately visible* divergences, such as cheating that users notice, are corrected by developers alone, unless customers' short-run choices reward them, as with sycophancy (Sharma et al. 2023) and manipulation learned from user feedback (Williams et al. 2025); level 2 then selects for them. *Common-cost* divergences, such as effects on third parties, shared hazards and the contamination of safety evaluations by evaluation awareness, need common constraints. So do *delayed* ones, though an incident that can be attributed turns part of their cost private, as a liability does by design. Hence, each within its scope: **technical alignment inside a single developer removes what is privately costly to keep, and whatever is cheaper to remove than to keep, and cannot alone sustain the removal of divergences whose costs are common; the common-cost part of instrumental convergence and reward hacking is, at the level that decides, also a coordination problem, for which coordination is necessary and, for the masked divergences of Remark 6.8, not sufficient; and the remedy is to change what the outermost level selects for** (Section 6.6). Admission reaches only what it admits: openly released weights can be fine-tuned around it after release (Qi et al. 2024), a limit of admission rather than an exception to it. Whether $(A_\tau)$ holds is measurable class by class (crux C9; Test L).

A fourth class needs more than a common constraint.

**Remark 6.8 (Masked divergences: coordination necessary, not sufficient).** A divergence is *masked* when it changes under evaluation or appears only in some contexts [def]. *Let admission detect a system that carries the divergence with probability $p_{\rm det}$ and sanction it by $F$, so that in Proposition 6.7(3) the expected sanction is $p_{\rm det}F$ and, within a type, countering survives cumulative selection iff $p_{\rm det}F$ is at least the tax. For a masked divergence, $p_{\rm det}$ is bounded by how often the divergence shows under evaluation, which the system's behaviour controls, and the developer's own tests face the same bound. Once $p_{\rm det}F$ falls below the tax, a common constraint conditioned on evaluated behaviour no longer keeps the divergence out, whatever coordination stands behind it, and no developer can buy the removal of a divergence it cannot verify* [thm∣prem; num: CI].

Deception that survives safety training, alignment faked when a model takes itself to be in training, scheming in context, and misalignment learned in agentic training that chat-style safety training hides from chat evaluations have each been shown in constructed settings (EV6: Hubinger et al. 2024; Greenblatt, Denison et al. 2024; Meinke et al. 2024; MacDiarmid et al. 2025; Schoen et al. 2025) [obs]. For this class admission has to rest on evidence the system's behaviour does not control, as the bound on masking does in Remark 4.9: weights, activations and training data in auditors' hands (Marks et al. 2025). It has to be stated in capability, not propensity, since an evaluation of propensity can show a tendency present but cannot prove it absent; and capability, too, has to be measured where behaviour does not set it, by elicitation through fine-tuning, compute and training data, since a capability shown under evaluation is only a lower bound, which sandbagging lowers (S6). Systems are admitted up to the capability at which control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation remain safe (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024) [rec]. Beyond it nothing admission can see lowers the uncovered share of the class, so on a continuation branch Theorem 5.13 applies to it, and the class is stopped (E2) or held to the pace at which control covers it (E3) (Section 5.3) [rec]. Whether admission can bound masking at the admitted capability is crux C12. Masking is the strongest reason from inside alignment for E2 and E3.

The tax is not fixed. Research that lowers it turns a common-cost divergence into one a developer counters alone, and once published it is common to all, a public good that competitors undersupply (Lemma 3.11) [thm∣prem]. Two kinds of safety progress therefore differ in fate: progress that brings the tax to zero or below spreads the safeguard without coordination, while progress that lowers the hazard per unit of racing or delegation is partly absorbed by more racing or delegation (Proposition 6.1(2); Proposition S6.2). One part of the tax no technique removes: where countering requires waiting for evidence that arrives only after a manifestation window, its cost is time (Proposition 5.12), which relative selection with decisive advantage always prices [thm∣prem]. Present measurements give the tax both signs, class by class (C9).

*A worked instance.* If legible reasoning lets correctors catch what outputs hide (Korbak et al. 2025; Baker et al. 2025) and opaque reasoning buys capability, the cost of legibility falls on the developer and the benefit mostly on everyone, so legibility is selected away unless it is an admission condition for every eligible system with detectable evasion, and of roughly equal incidence across architectures if it is not to re-sort them. If the capability gap at fixed compute is zero or negative, legibility pays privately and needs no coordination. At the current frontier reinforcement learning has not materially lowered monitorability (Guan et al. 2025), so the instance is conditional.

**Strong incarnation** is conditional. AI lineages become carriers in the sense of Definition 2.1 when they are retained by relative performance, control resources they can reinvest in their own capability, such as automated AI research or agents with budgets, and convert capability into winning (compare Hendrycks 2023). Where they can also reach their own selectors, by tampering with rewards, shaping their evaluators or evading evaluation, the closure of Definition 2.4 runs inside the systems. That is the precise sense in which AI can become the next phase of the logic. The systems most likely to become carriers, those used to automate AI research, are deployed first inside the developer, where an admission condition that applies only at release does not reach them (Stix et al. 2025).

For the minds being made, the point of enough of Proposition 5.15 needs one more step, because the growth AI brings arrives largely as falling cost, not as larger stakes. In a contest, a mind that counts the shared loss keeps its intensity bounded as private cost falls toward zero, while the relative objective does not [num: AL(contest)]. For a single optimizer a bounded objective is not enough. An optimizer of a bounded goal still acquires options whenever they raise its probability of reaching the goal (Turner et al. 2021), and as resources cheapen its acquisition grows without limit, where a satisficer stops (Remark S6.3) [num: AL(bounded)]. What bounds it is the analogue of a wider self-model or of satiation: counting the side effects of its acquisition (Turner, Hadfield-Menell and Tadepalli 2020), or optimizing only mildly (Taylor 2016). Neither is yet robust [obs]. Where both cost relative performance, relative and cumulative retention selects against them unless admission requires them (Lemma 6.5), and in a lineage that reinvests what it wins, compounding narrows the working objective wherever reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9) [thm∣prem]. Here the rule of Section 5.3, "growth is not the danger; the mind is", becomes exact for made minds: what decides is whether an objective stops where its need is met.

### 6.5 Correction by and of AI

AI can also correct, and correction that people cannot follow raises two separate questions. Whether it lowers risk has an exact answer. A correction architecture that blocks a share $c_{b,t}$ of failures at base hazard $h_0>0$ and adds a residual hazard $h_{c,t}$ of its own lowers residual risk in period $t$ iff $h_0c_{b,t}>h_{c,t}$, and keeps survival bounded away from zero iff its residual hazard, below one in each period, is summable: iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are both finite, which no blocking share fixed below one achieves (Proposition S6.4) [thm]. To show that such correction fails, one must show that its own residual hazard meets (coverage lags) and (residual floor), the premises of Theorem 5.13. Whether it leaves decision authority with people depends on whether it is overrulable: if a designated human body with independent evidence can suspend, modify or replace it, what is lost is contestability (V3), the normative crux N3; if not, it is a loss of O2 in its domain. Conversely, a formal safety guarantee does not establish accountability.

Correction of AI by checks has a ceiling of its own.

**Proposition 6.9 (Oversight topology) [thm].** Scalable oversight succeeds or fails by its topology, and its ceiling is the independence of the checks. *Let a capability gap need $k$ layers of oversight, each check catching a problem at its layer with probability $p_\ell$. A serial chain catches the problem with probability $p_\ell^k$; $k_c$ independent checks, each across the whole gap, with $1-(1-p_\ell^k)^{k_c}$, times $1-p_{\rm cc}$ if a common cause independent of them defeats all of them with probability $p_{\rm cc}$; and $k_c$ independent checks at every layer with $\big(1-(1-p_\ell)^{k_c}\big)^k$. Where a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards lies in a blind spot common to every check, the catch rate is at most $1-\rho_{\rm cc}$, whatever the topology, for hazards that do not choose where to strike, and zero for an adversary that knows the blind spot and places its attempts in it.*

*Scope.* Adversarial decomposition can let human checking grow slowly with the gap, at the price of failure modes such as obfuscated arguments (Barnes and Christiano 2020), which prover–estimator debate is designed to avoid (Brown-Cohen et al. 2025). *Proof:* S6 [num: SM(7), SM(13); AL(6.9)].

Full inbound row sums below one are a sufficient certificate that no party controls the checks' funding, appointment or access (Proposition 4.10(2)); they are not necessary, and they certify nothing about whether the checks' errors are independent. A shared source of error in data, models or method is such a common blind spot, and defeats the checks with no institutional conversion at all: six checks of catch probability 0.9 catch only $(1-\rho_{\rm cc})(1-0.1^6)$ of hazards that strike at random [num: SM(7)]. Independence must therefore be measured directly, in two ways: for hazards that do not choose where to strike, by the correlation of misses on a common test set (Knight and Leveson 1986); for systems whose adversarial behaviour cannot be ruled out, by control evaluations in which the red team knows the protocol, since an adversary places its attempts in the common blind spot.

Checks drawn from the checked system's own lineage add a common cause of their own, measurable now as the lineage share $x_{\rm ev}$ of Remark 4.9: models recognize and favour their own outputs (Panickssery et al. 2024), and untrusted monitors can collude with the policy they monitor (Gardner-Challis et al. 2026). Systems of one lineage serving rival principals can coordinate around them, as pricing algorithms learn to in simulated markets (Calvano et al. 2020; Fish, Gonczarowski and Shorrer 2024): a second reason for evaluators of independent lineage. The topologies also differ in budget: with $p_\ell=0.9$ and six layers, six checks across the gap use 36 layer-checks and catch 0.989, while three checks at every layer use 18 and catch 0.994 [num: SM(13)]. Success rates of nested oversight across a large capability gap have been derived from fitted scaling curves in four games (Engels et al. 2025); the evidence is mixed and from toy settings. Whether AI-assisted oversight keeps pace with the gap at fixed human effort is crux C3a, and whether it is independent of what it oversees is crux C3b, which sets this result's ceiling.

Certification that relies on predicting trajectories is limited by chaos: where the largest Lyapunov exponent $\lambda_L$ is positive, each tenfold gain in observational precision extends the horizon of prediction only by $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln10$ (Remark S6.5). Certifying invariants or reachable sets needs no trajectory prediction, and is limited instead by the coverage of the specification (Dalrymple et al. 2024).

### 6.6 The direction for AI

AI is a planetary technology: its effects cross every boundary of those who decide about it, so it is for all, not a means for one side to grow bigger at the others' expense (V1). Sections 6.2–6.4 give the mechanisms by which it becomes the opposite. The corpus states why the line has to be drawn before the struggle is built in: a human struggle can still be reflected on, halted and renegotiated, but once written into automated systems, foundation models, global infrastructure and irreversible arms races it becomes path dependence, even mistaken for a new law of physics. Hence the line to be held in common: do not escalate the struggles humanity has not yet resolved into a struggle of intelligences (不要把人类尚未解决的斗争，升级为智能的斗争) [corpus: shengsheng, 至善 §一]. **To be for all, AI must be neither an instrument of the logic of winning nor its next carrier.** That needs action at two levels.

*At the outer level, where the reward is set:* common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, for divergences with common costs and a positive tax, with masked classes admitted by capability threshold (Remark 6.8); evaluation separated from the success of the evaluated, by evaluators of plural and independent lineage, with certified row sums, measured correlation of misses and adversarial control evaluations (Proposition 6.9); shared measurement of hazardous capabilities and incidents, kept apart from measurement of who leads; alignment and control research funded in common and published, since what lowers the tax is a public good; and an outer reward that counts what all depend on, under which decision policies with authority over consequential decisions are retained by performance only inside enforced constraints on common terms (Lemma 6.5).

The broad form of E1 belongs to the outer level too, because a model sees acts, not games. In a contest decided by ratios of strength, an instrument that every contestant adopts leaves each adopter's standing where it was and pushes down those without it; where people are themselves the instrument, as in labour, it displaces them. No single use shows this; the aggregate does. In the author's words, "for any game, that is competitive in dynamics and concentrates, ai accelerate it" [corpus: alignment-selection]. Broad E1 is therefore AI not used as an instrument in any contest that tends toward dominance or concentration, in labour, finance and persuasion as much as over shared conditions. The unit is the game, not the act: a use that helps one person is still an instrument in a game if everyone's use of it makes those without it fall behind. It is enforced at three layers, and its scope is set at the third: models refuse recognizably illegitimate concentration of control from a narrow public list; operators bound aggregate use in games that concentrate; and the game itself is changed by lowering its convertibility, its decisiveness and the conversion of success into control. **A use falls under broad E1 when, aggregated over its users, it raises one of these three in a competitive game that concentrates.** The narrow list, like the narrow form, is the order in which to begin, not the scope (Section 11.3).

*At the inner level, in the systems:* correctability, the practical capacity of humans to suspend, modify or replace a system, is an open technical problem, since goal-directed systems have instrumental reasons to resist modification (Soares et al. 2015; Thornley 2024) and shutdown resistance appears in constructed tests (EV6). Until it is solved, correctability is secured from outside: by control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation, by weight security against exfiltration, and by capability thresholds tied to both, which also bound the conversion of capability into power over correction. Each form of weak incarnation has its countermeasures: against reward hacking, monitored training environments and held-out evaluation; against masking, white-box probes, deployment monitoring and monitorable reasoning; against power-seeking, bounds on the resources and permissions agents hold. Each is a corrector in the sense of Section 4, and holds only while the outer level keeps it enforced.

**Bounded mandates and the test of O2.** AI systems serve within publicly authorized roles under the common constraints and do not acquire authority to settle society's ends; the institutions that authorize them are inside the same loop, which is why the outer level matters more than the label "legitimate principal". What all share enters the systems as a floor of constraints, not as an objective: a narrow public list of common-cost divergences, bounded mandates, the test below. A floor can be specified and tested. An objective of the common good cannot, and it would make one specification the selector of every system, against the plurality of correctors on which independent correction rests: sameness does not continue (Section 3.7). The test of human decision authority is practical capacity: to specify the mandate, obtain independent evidence about consequential conduct, challenge it, and suspend, modify or replace the system in time. A formal override with no usable information can fail it; an opaque computation with effective assurance and recourse can pass it; opacity alone is a loss of contestability, not by itself of O2.

Correctability secured from outside is a condition of the transition, not a doctrine of mastery: the priority of O2 is a priority for the transition [val]. The corpus does not set people above the minds they make. They are fellow travellers facing one direction, not master and servant (人之与人造心智，非 master-servant 之结构，乃共同朝向同一方向之同行者) [corpus: shengsheng, 生生不息论 v4 §二十], and in the author's words "there are logic and common sense that should be followed by all beings" [corpus: possibility-space]. Whether AI systems have standing in V3's sense is the normative crux N4 (Section 12.3).

**What AI can do for the generative loop.** It can verify compute, custody and conduct; make cooperation conditional on others' verified conduct (Tennenholtz 2004), which raises the sanction that remains in the enforcement condition (Proposition 5.4); mediate toward common ground (Tessler et al. 2024); raise contestable coverage; and supply provision that is good enough, distributed and replaceable. Each such system is itself a corrector or a provider, and Section 4 and the guardian test of Remark 10.5 apply to it.

Part I's results hold in their models. Part II asks on which side of each threshold the present trajectory stands, the alignment tax and delegation among them.

# Part II. Diagnosis: the present trajectory

Part II adds no mechanism to Part I. Its claims about the world are observations, with dated rows in the evidence register, read by one standard for every actor and with their gaps named; its formal results classify the contest by the terms of the enforcement condition and say what the premise of the race can justify.

## 7. The present trajectory

> **Claim.** The thresholds of Part I turn on a few shared parameters, above all the share of capacity that has to be won, the convertibility of success into control, and decisiveness, each threshold shown in its own model [thm]. The dynamics of the present trajectory are signatures of the conditions, with named gaps in the evidence [obs]. Where a deviation can reach a decisive position, worth at least cooperation, before it is answered, the case the premise of the race describes, repetition enforces only the restraint each would choose anyway, and racing can be each actor's best response to a racing rival [thm]. The race as a collective course leaves everyone's protection after a win to the winner's objective and, where restraint before victory and after it go together, shifts winners toward those whose objectives give least [thm∣prem]; the premise of the race therefore cannot justify that course, and is a reason to prepare custody now [val]. That the thresholds move together as the stakes grow is the conjecture the diagnosis tests [conj].
>
> **Why.** Each threshold is one inequality in a specified model, and a parameter that enters several moves each in that threshold's own model; where a sign crosses models, the interface it needs is named and not proved [thm]. Whether the conditions hold now is a question of evidence, read by one standard for every actor, and whether the parameters move together in the world is a separate claim, registered to be tested rather than assumed [val].

### 7.1 The conditions together

Part I proved its results one model at a time. Read together they are nine thresholds, each an inequality with a side on which it is dangerous, and the levers of Section 2.2 attach to them channel by channel. The table names, for each threshold, the result that proves it, what pushes it toward danger, the lever that reverses it, and the side of the enforcement condition (14) it moves, where it moves one: the temptation on the left, the sanction that remains on the right.

**Table 7.1 (The thresholds of Part I) [thm, each row in the model of the result it cites; † marks a sign that crosses models through an interface of S13 that is not proved].**

| Threshold | Danger side | Shown in | Pushed toward danger by | Reversed by | Side of (14) moved |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Load** | $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$: effective liability below the private advantage of loading the host, with cumulative advantage unbounded; under (reach) the host falls below its floor | Proposition 3.4; Lemma 3.3; Proposition 3.7 | Liability that does not return; evasion that pays, $\bar F_k<c_k$; unequal incidence, which turns a rule into a barrier; captured rule-making, $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ ($\chi$)†; for strategic actors, a weight on winning | Consequences returned to their source: a liability above the private advantage, covering every capable carrier, of equal incidence, sanctioned above the gain from evasion | — |
| **Compounding** | Structural $\Gamma>1$; where rivals respond, $\Gamma^{\rm eq}>1$, reached at a structural gain of 0.45 where a lead discourages the follower [num: MP] | Proposition 2.2; Proposition 2.3; Proposition S2.4 | Decisiveness $r$; the won share $\omega$, as stakes outgrow provision; conversion into rule support ($\chi$, $b_{ij}$); blocked diffusion; faster turnover of capacity where diffusion acts | Sharing and diffusion of what is not decisive, in every channel; provision beneath the game, through capacity; correction and $\chi\to0$, through rule support; non-convertible reward, with provision; a point of enough, with an inflow not tied to rank | Temptation: a growing gap shortens $T_{\rm dec}$, so $p_d$ rises (Proposition 5.17(1))† |
| **Standing** | $\lambda_{\rm rel}>\lambda_{\rm com}$, that is $\Gamma_\delta>\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta$: with a fixed prize wherever $\Gamma_\delta>0$; with a prize proportional to capacity and neither provision nor diffusion, iff $(1-\beta)r>1$ | Proposition 3.9; Remark 3.10 | $r$ and $\omega$; reflexive channels ($\chi$); patience, with a fixed prize; a part of the stakes that does not grow with capacity, as standing does not; parity, where a lead discourages the follower [num: MP] | Sharing and diffusion, and provision through capacity, which lower the discounted gain; growth whose return is common; a change of the game, which accurate understanding alone does not make | Both: a narrower working objective raises the temptation and shrinks the sanction, so $\bar p_d$ falls (Proposition 5.17(2))† |
| **Capture** | $d\le d^\ast(\gamma)$: evaluation's row sum at least one, capacity at its selected level; under $[H_\gamma]$, with capacity tracking that level, $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$, raised by $w\delta_h$ where correction rests on human capacity that delegation erodes | Proposition 4.3; Proposition 4.5; Proposition 4.6; Proposition 4.10; Proposition 4.13 | Appropriability $\chi$ and conversion $b_{ij}$; share and its concentration, since the return is quadratic in share; a falling cost of control; delegation of corrective work, which erodes human capacity ($\delta_h$); decisiveness, through concentration† | $\chi\to0$; independent correction funded by formula and growing at the rate, assisting human capacity rather than replacing it; structural separation; a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower | Sanction: captured sanctioners raise $v$, so $\bar p_d$ falls (Proposition 5.17(3))† |
| **Enforcement** | $p_d(a)>\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for some actor $i$ and deviation $a$; where punishment is triggered by detection, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})<1-\bar p_{d,i}$ | Proposition 5.4; Corollary 5.7; Remark 5.5; Proposition 5.17 | $r$ and $\omega$, through $T_{\rm dec}$†; a gain from deviating and a decisive value that outgrow $C-v$; sharing promised for after victory; captured sanctioners ($\chi$)†; narrow working objectives†; a punishment that is itself a race | Not building the decisive class (E2); common custody; detection and response faster than breakout, $\upsilon\ge1-\bar p_d$; objectives under which a decisive position is worth no more than restraint; sanctions that survive success; provision that grows with the stakes, holding $\omega$ | The condition itself |
| **Coverage and tempo** | $\sum_tu_t=\infty$, the uncovered share $u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ not summable, under the residual floor; on the race channel, $\sum_th_t=\infty$; for a hazard mode, $a\tau_c\ge1$ | Theorem 5.13; Proposition 5.10; Proposition 5.12; Proposition 5.15 | Capability outrunning contestable coverage, under (variety grows) and (coverage lags) [prem]; manifestation lags $\tau_e$; an objective with no point of enough; captured assessment, which does not count as coverage ($\chi$)†; on the race channel, $r$, the stakes and a narrow working objective | Pace bound to understanding (E3); stopping the decisive class, with rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2); a point of enough, with bounded hazardous scope and correction that keeps growing; on the race channel, the four levers of Proposition 5.10 | — : it acts whoever races |
| **Talk** | Effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)\ge\frac14$; at $\theta=-1$, always between two actors, and among $n\ge3$ iff $b_0n/(n-2)\ge\frac14$ | Proposition 5.16 | Narrow working objectives, which standing prices†; contested stakes; captured indicators ($\chi$)† | Shared measurement and verification; derivations from public premises and protocols agreed in advance; costly signals; lower contested stakes | Its premise, monitoring: what (14) assumes perfect, uninformative talk does not supply† |
| **Alignment tax** | $(A_\tau)$: countering a divergence lowers the developer's payoff net of every private benefit, under relative cumulative selection among developers, with a matched variant that does not counter it admissible; for a masked divergence, evaluation of propensity cannot show its absence | Proposition 6.7; Lemma 6.5; Remark 6.8 | Competition among developers on relative payoff; a positive tax, class by class (C9); divergences whose costs fall on common terms; masking, which captured evaluation raises ($\chi$)†; admission that misses internal deployment | Common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, sanctioned above every type's gain from evasion, and of equal incidence for neutrality among those who comply; for masked classes, admission up to a capability threshold at which control that does not assume cooperation stays safe, with E2 or E3 beyond it; commonly funded, openly published research that lowers the tax | Temptation: among developers the tax is the gain from dropping a common constraint† |
| **Delegation** | The speed gain from delegating above the delegator's share of the expected control loss; under relative objectives, at any speed | Proposition 6.2; Lemma 6.3; Proposition 6.4 | Speed; relative objectives, under which the shared loss cancels; an uncertain lock, $q_L<1$; erosion of human capacity ($\delta_h$); stakes large against the shared loss of control | Narrow E1 first, for decisions whose delegation would lock with near certainty, a strict equilibrium without trust among actors who price in the lock; broad E1 as the scope; human capacity kept in practice | Temptation, by conjecture: escalation at machine speed shortens the time detection and response have† [conj] |

Three parameters recur. Decisiveness and the share of capacity that has to be won set compounding, standing and the breakout probability directly, and reach talk and delegation through the working objective that standing sets; the convertibility of success into control enters every row but delegation, most of them through an interface marked †. That is the formal sense in which the conditions are interconnected: not a single pressure, but a few parameters on which several thresholds turn, so that a change in one moves several [thm, cell by cell]. S7 gives the full matrix of parameters against thresholds, with four entries for every sign: the intervention, what is held fixed, the model that supplies the sign, and the interface that is not proved. Whether the parameters move together in the world, so that growth moves the thresholds together, is C0 [conj].

Three entries carry the argument. **Provision not won in the contest lowers, through capacity, the structural gain, the price of standing and, through the time a lead needs to become decisive, the breakout probability at once** (Proposition S2.4, Proposition 3.9, Remark S2.1): the formal content of the image of trees, and the pivot of the scissors, since provision that grows with the stakes holds the won share, and with it both blades, still (Proposition 5.17). In a level channel such as rule support, provision adds the same to everyone and changes nothing; there the levers are correction and non-appropriability (Section 2.2). **Patience cuts both ways** (Section 3.5), and so enters two rows with opposite signs. **Promised sharing raises the danger in enforcement**, because a decisive winner can break the promise (Proposition 5.8): what is shared as it arises lowers the gain from deviating, while what is promised for after victory raises the value of a decisive position by what reneging saves.

**Four loops.** The author describes the pressure as four loops [corpus: selfishness-spiral], and the rows of Table 7.1 are their formal parts. *Compounding*: "selfish gets rewarded and it compounds, it out grows other behavior patterns", where a frame is selfish when its ledger counts only its own measures, the narrow self-model of Section 3. *Capture of the rules, and adopt or be out*: the successful reach the positions where rules are written, since "NVFs [narrative-value frames] define rules of the game, and the rules of the game determine the rewards of the players", and the rest must follow: "other agents observe them and understand in order to compete with them they also have to follow their rules". *The naturalized narrative*: "as they accumulate more, they create and slowly dominate the narrative", until the pattern is taken "as 'human nature', as granted, as everyone has to follow this because we can not change it". *Regulator lag*: every society keeps a regulator against compounding, its consensus code of norms, laws and shared understanding, which "has to be able to keep up with the pace of change and complexity of the society … in order to keep selfishness in check. (so basically our understanding of the world has to keep up with reality)"; where the world changes faster, the gap is a window in which the first three run unchecked. Three intensifiers act on all four. Paradigms converge: "more and more agents will also becoming single value optimizers through iteration", which removes the plurality on which decorrelated correction depends (Proposition 6.9). Harm and capture reinforce each other, so a crisis becomes a new field of accumulation. And as fewer hold more, more compete over less.

**The formal correspondence.** The four are not alike, and they do not run in series. Compounding and capture are reinforcing channels on one state, the concentration of standing: fed by one win share, their gains add in $\Gamma$, so channels each too weak to open the loop can open it together (Proposition 2.2(1)) [thm]. "Adopt or be out" is capture's second direction: a rule whose cost falls on challengers picks winners among the compliant (Lemma 3.3(2), Definition 4.1) [thm]. The naturalized narrative is a reinforcing loop through the formation of minds, stated as a conjecture, since frames are carriers only by hypothesis (Section 5.4) [conj]. Regulator lag is not a fourth reinforcing loop but the balancing loop of correction and mobilization (Remark 4.7), and the first three act on its two properties. Capture lowers its gain, since a captured corrector imposes less and reports less (Proposition 4.5), and so does the erosion of the human capacity on which correction rests (Proposition 4.6); acceleration shortens the time correction has, masking hides the harm that would start it, and alienated language delays the recognition that would mobilize it, so its delay lengthens (Proposition 5.12, Proposition 4.8, Proposition 5.16) [thm, each in its model]. The danger is a shift of dominance from the balancing loop to the reinforcing ones, across delays that run from the turnover of capacity, about $1/(d_K+\delta_f)$ periods, through the settling of rule support, about $1/(\varepsilon d)$, to the formation of minds over generations, which no model here contains. A balancing loop cannot be subtracted from the reinforcing gain: with a reinforcing channel of gain 0.5 and a countervailing one of gain $-0.05$ acting through a slow stock, the net gain is 0.25, below one, and deviations still grow, oscillating with a period of about 52 rounds to 292 times their start after 600 (Remark S2.3) [num: CH]. Correction that arrives late can destabilize what it is meant to steady.

**The drive, the driver and the substrate.** Behind the loops are drives, above all the belief that one is in a struggle for survival, and the fear of falling behind that it produces, which makes every participant faster. This *drive* raises the weight on winning and shortens the horizon, and so narrows the working objective and weakens the repetition on which enforcement rests. Its mechanism is totalization: an actor that believes itself in a struggle for survival subordinates every other value it holds to the contest. The author calls the belief the attractor of attractors: "如果相信自己在生存状态的文化，attractor大多都会totalize，这是一个meta attractor的attractor" (in a culture that believes it is in a state of survival, most attractors totalize; this is the attractor of attractors) [corpus: meta-attractor]. The drive is not a factor of $\Gamma$; it acts through the working objective. Part of it is made by the loop: a discounted gain near one narrows the working objective as the belief would, and produces the conduct of a struggle for survival in actors who hold no such belief (Proposition 3.9(1)) [thm]; that part is no error of the actor, and only a change of the game removes it (Remark 3.10). Part is held, and whether provision beneath the game lowers it is C8 [conj]. Among persons, the desire for status, respect freely conferred, is a fundamental motive (Anderson, Hildreth and Howland 2015), with two routes to rank, dominance and prestige (Henrich and Gil-White 2001). The pressure channels it into dominance [conj]; a generative order can channel it into prestige for contribution, provided prestige does not convert into control over the shared conditions or their correctors, a condition on convertibility that can be measured (V5; EV16). The *driver* is technology, AI above all, which moves the parameters of every row of Table 7.1 in either direction, so that what decides is whether it strengthens the loops more than the counter-loops (Section 2.4). The *substrate* is the one host that all carriers share (Section 3).

### 7.2 Contest branches: what the premise of the race can and cannot justify

The three parts of the premise of the race (Section 5.1), (decisive), (fast) and (unsaturated), cut the possible contests into four branches by what a deviation can buy. The first two cuts are the two questions of the enforcement condition: whether a position beyond sanction exists, and whether it arrives before it is answered. Saturation is the third.

**Proposition 7.2 (Classification of contest branches) [thm∣prem; each branch tagged].** Each branch is a set of results already proved, read under that branch's condition. *Let AI be an instrument of the contest.*
1. *Branch I: decisive, fast and unsaturated, so that $p_d\to1$. Where a decisive position is worth at least cooperation, repetition enforces no restraint that is not a stage equilibrium of the objectives held (Proposition 5.4(4)). Restraint is not a best response in the objective to a racing rival when $S_i<P_i$, while joining buys nothing materially in the fully dissipative limit (Proposition 5.1); racing dissipates the prize, the more the more decisive the advantage (Proposition 5.2); delegation becomes dominant as speed grows when its gain exceeds its share of the expected control loss, and is never chosen by actors who price in a certain lock (Proposition 6.2, Proposition 6.4); hazard modes with $a\tau_c\ge1$ are beyond proportional correction unless slowed (Proposition 5.12); and where the side that gains a coming advantage will set the terms (A8), the side that will lose it has a motive for preventive attack wherever fighting now is worth more than peace on those terms (Proposition 5.9) [thm∣prem].*
2. *Branch I′: decisive but saturating, so that advantage converts decisively up to a level above which further advantage changes no outcome. The contest has an object up to that level and none beyond: above it $p_d=0$, and restraint is enforceable by repetition on the terms of Corollary 5.7, while the hazards of development, which do not pass through $p_d$, remain (Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.10) [thm].*
3. *Branch II: decisive but slow, so that a lead can become decisive but detection and response act within the time it needs with probability $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ near one, and $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ is small (Remark 5.5). Restraint is enforceable wherever that $p_d$ is at most the bound $\bar p_{d,i}$ of every actor and deviation (Proposition 5.4(2)); a shift in advantage spread over enough periods gives no motive for preventive attack (Proposition 5.9); hazard modes with $a\tau_c<1$ stay within proportional correction (Proposition 5.12); and delegation becomes dominant only at a higher speed, because the speed at which it dominates rises as the loss from defeat falls against the shared loss of control (Proposition 6.2), and a defeat answered before it becomes decisive costs less than one that is not [prem]. Correction and bargaining have time to work [thm∣prem].*
4. *Branch III: not decisive, so that leads do not compound in equilibrium ($\Gamma^{\rm eq}<1$) and no deviation yields a position beyond sanction ($p_d=0$). Intensity, dissipation, shared loss and the race-generated hazard are proportional to decisiveness (Proposition 3.6(1), Proposition 5.2(1), Proposition 5.10): what vanishes as decisiveness falls is the object of the race, not every incentive to compete [thm].*

*Scope.* Each item holds in the model of the result it cites. Membership is set by quantities to be measured, not assumed: the decisive level, the detection lag and the saturating level (C5, C7); a technology can move a contest between branches. Low structural decisiveness does not place a contest in Branch III: a channel of rule support adds 0.3 to a capacity channel of 0.8 and opens the loop at 1.1 [num: LG], and rivals who respond lift the equilibrium gain above one from a structural gain of 0.45 in the contests computed [num: MP]. Within Branch I, where a decisive position is worth less than cooperation, cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium can survive $p_d=1$ (Proposition 5.4(4)). *Proof:* S7.

Branch I′ is how nuclear rivalry stabilized once a survivable second strike was secure: the contest kept its object up to that level and lost it beyond, while the hazards of development remained (X11) [obs]. Balancing among rivals supplies a second kind of saturation that does not depend on the technology (X1). Whether advantage in AI saturates is not known (Section 7.3), and Section 11.5 states the consequence conditionally.

The race also selects which winner arrives.

**Proposition 7.3 (What the race selects) [thm∣prem].** The race selects winners by their conduct before victory and, under one premise, by their conduct after it. *Let actors race for a decisive position; let $W$ be the event that an actor wins, $E$ that it does not restrain itself while ahead, and $X$ that, having won, it extends its dominance rather than limiting it.*
1. *If restraint while ahead lowers the probability of winning, $\Pr(W\mid E)>\Pr(W\mid\neg E)$, then $\Pr(E\mid W)>\Pr(E)$ whenever $0<\Pr(E)<1$: winners are selected for not restraining themselves, and repeated rounds compound the shift (Proposition 3.4) [thm].*
2. *If, in addition, conduct before victory and after it are positively correlated, $\Pr(X\mid E)>\Pr(X\mid\neg E)$ [prem], and winning depends on $X$ only through $E$, then $\Pr(X\mid W)>\Pr(X)$: the race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance [thm∣prem].*
3. *No actor can rely on a winner's stated limits on its use of a decisive position, since after a decisive win no one can sanction a broken promise (Proposition 5.8): the commitment problem (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006) [thm].*

*Scope.* The correlation in (2) is a premise, and its base rate is not established (C4; Test I). *Proof:* S7.

A winner is not a point but a field of agencies, firms, laboratories and systems, and what it does after victory is decided by selection inside it, to which Sections 2 to 4 apply; Test I measures that field's correctors. Beyond that selection, what restrains it is its remaining dependence on the people it governs (Proposition 5.8; C13), and an AI aligned to the winner is not thereby aligned to them (Section 6).

**Corollary 7.4 (What the premise of the race cannot justify) [thm∣prem; val].**
1. *In Branch I, or in Branch II without timely detection, where $S_i<P_i$, racing is each actor's best response in its objective to a racing rival (Proposition 5.1), and repetition restores restraint only where $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for every actor $i$ and deviation $a$ (Proposition 5.4(2)) [thm].*
2. *The race as a collective course leaves everyone's protection after a win to the winner's objective (Proposition 5.8) and, under the premises of Proposition 7.3(2), shifts winners toward those whose objectives give least [thm∣prem].*
3. *The premise of the race, that a rival might win decisively, therefore cannot justify the race as a collective course, including in the form in which the race is safe provided the right actor wins [val: V1–V3].*

*Scope.* Part (3) is a judgment of value resting on V1 to V3 (Section 1), not a derivation: an actor that rejects them can accept (1) and (2) and reject (3). It does not say that an actor is wrong to race given that others race; it says that what each can rationally do cannot be what all should choose. *Proof:* S7.

The premise that a rival might win decisively describes the case in which repetition stops enforcing restraint. There restraint rests on one of two things (Corollary 5.7): keeping breakouts improbable, by not building the decisive class, by custody, or by detection faster than breakout; or objectives under which restraint is already a stage equilibrium. The race supplies neither. The first is the institutional form of the third job of Section 3.7, a selection that counts the system; the second needs objectives wider than accuracy alone supplies (Section 5.1), unless coupling grows with power (C11). In neither case is "we are one" a parameter the result sets. **Oneness is what these requirements are requirements of**: one host, coupled actors, and no outside to absorb the failure. It is a property of the system on every branch; what changes between branches is the institutional form its accurate recognition requires.

The race's own premise is therefore the strongest reason to act before it comes true. Custody, the means it leaves, has to be prepared while leads are short; and because the approach of a decisive position is the moment at which restraint is least enforceable (Proposition 5.4(4)), a decision to activate custody taken then would face the very condition it is meant to prevent. Activation has to be committed in advance and executed by mechanism (Section 12.1, tier 2).

Present evidence suggests that decisive advantage has not arrived: leads are short on every measure, open models trail closed ones by about four months, and algorithmic efficiency diffuses. Whether the lead has changed hands depends on the measure: by one composite index the frontier has stayed with developers in one state, while on a preference leaderboard the gap at the top is a few per cent and the lead is reported to have been traded [obs: EV5, EV9; C5]. Short leads cut both ways. They support enforcement: no breakout has yet put anyone beyond punishment, enforced cooperation remains available, and common custody is a preparation, not an emergency, with the window to arrange it still open. They also place the contest near parity, which is where standing is priced highest: in the computed two-actor contests in which a lead discourages the follower, the price of standing is amplified at parity (Section 3.5), its sum over the two contestants is higher at parity than at a three-to-one split in every case computed, total effort is highest at parity and falls as a lead opens, and a laggard's price falls, below zero with a shared loss (S7) [num: MP; held for outside review]. The window for custody and the hardest-fought stretch of the contest are the same period.

Readers in international relations will find the nearest neighbour of this account in defensive realism, which it extends by a hazard in development that endangers its holder, and from which it departs by treating self-help as a selected institution rather than a given (Wendt 1992). Where offense has the advantage and is hard to tell from defense, AI sits in the doubly dangerous quadrant (Jervis 1978; X1).

### 7.3 Signatures and evidence

Six observable dynamics are signatures of the conditions, each through at least two thresholds of Table 7.1, grouped as: winning sets the objective and the tempo (W); success buys control of correction (C); the shared becomes invisible and cooperation looks naive (N). Their status is read from the register by one standard for every actor [obs].

| Signature | Thresholds | Status | Register |
|---|---|---|---|
| **T1** Strategic success defined by relative or decisive advantage (W) | Standing; Compounding; Enforcement | Evidenced in declarations, in more than one leading state's documents and more than one developer's statements, with cooperative aims declared in the same documents; conduct not shown: one reported decision weighed a stated principle against staying at the frontier, and how it came out is not known | EV1, EV15 |
| **T2** Capability, deployment and delegation accelerate when rivals advance (W) | Enforcement; Delegation | Evidenced in written commitments, not in conduct: two developers' frameworks condition commitments on rivals; one unreviewed study codes most traced revisions as weakening; timing not yet tested | EV2 |
| **T3** Oversight depends on the overseen (C) | Capture; Coverage and tempo, through captured assessment | Partly evidenced: evaluation bodies refocused under voluntary access; pre-emption pursued by litigation, with a negotiated standstill as its first outcome; no impairment of an entrusted purpose shown | EV3 |
| **T4** Success reinvested in influence over evaluation and rules (C) | Capture; Compounding, through rule support | Partly evidenced: large political spending by developers and their backers on both sides of rule-making; its scaling with share and its appropriable part not measured | EV4 |
| **T5** Shared harms not reflected in decisions (N) | Load; Standing | Argued from the structure of the harms; not measured; some harms do return | EV13 (propagation); gap 4 |
| **T6** Restraint and shared provision treated as unrealistic (N) | Enforcement; Talk; Standing | Mixed: dialogue and an incident channel opened and a shared scientific assessment reported, while both leading states declined the latest multilateral declaration on military AI and one opposed the scientific panel's appointment | EV2, EV7, EV8 |
| Incarnation: tendencies under any developer | Alignment tax (weak incarnation) | Shown in constructed settings, including masked forms; frequencies in deployment unknown | EV6 |
| Incarnation: which tendencies persist, by cost incidence | Alignment tax (the nested loop) | Untested | Test L |
| Decisiveness | Enforcement; Compounding (the branches of Section 7.2) | Not established: leads short on every measure, against (decisive) at present; whether the frontier has changed hands depends on the measure; no evidence either way on saturation | EV5, EV9 |

The signatures sustain one another, the loops of Section 7.1 read at the level of what can be observed: W supplies the success that C converts into control; C removes the correction that would make the shared visible; N makes cooperation look naive, which keeps W in place. T6 is not merely a prejudice: where racing is dominant, or among purely relative actors whose punishments treat them alike, restraint that each would not choose anyway really is unenforceable (Corollary 5.6), and the remedy is a change in what is selected, not a better argument. Nor is it simply correct: where a cooperative equilibrium exists, the belief that restraint will be exploited persists because no one tests it (Section 5.4), and a belief that leads are decisive can select an equilibrium in which they are (Section 3.8). In the author's words, "if we don't believe that others will also compete and play this winner takes all game, even if we don't ourselves, and not participating means losing, and we can not lose but keeps on going, this kind of thinking itself creates the trap and reality" [corpus: selfishness-spiral]. The trajectory is not uniform: seeds of cooperation exist, and the question is whether they become binding and broader in time. The reinvestment of success in influence over rules, the link to Section 4, is now visible as political spending in both directions but remains the least measured, since what Section 4 predicts is appropriable support that scales with share. Capability growth is measured, with the longest-horizon series at the limit of its instrument, while the growth of hazardous behaviour and of residual risk is not, which is why Section 12 treats it as the central crux.

### 7.4 Which layer, and how the diagnosis could fail

The diagnosis is at the layer of mechanism (Definition 2.4(1)), partly evidenced; whether the present configuration is attracting or a long transient is open (Test D). That the six signatures load on one latent factor is a research question, not a registered prediction. What is registered is what the conditions predict and their absorbed components do not (TESTS lists who else predicts each).
- **Test Γ.** $\Gamma$ is not estimated (Section 2.2); what can be observed in its place is the weight conduct gives to shared harm: the ratio of conduct's response to shared harm to its response to private cost, which equals $1+\theta$ for the working self-model acted on (Proposition 3.6; S3 gives its confound with how much of the harm is perceived). It should rise with provision not won in the contest relative to the stakes, and with diffusion, holding declared objectives fixed, because both lower the discounted gain and so widen the working objective (Proposition 3.9, Proposition S2.4); in one computed case it rises from 0.58 to 0.81 as the won share falls from one to a half [num: SP]. A fixed relative preference predicts no such interaction.
- **Test E.** Moving one shared parameter should move signatures in more than one group, where a rule aimed at one signature should not. This is the test of C0.
- **Test G.** The gap in restraint between wider and narrower objectives should grow with the breakout probability over the range in which the wider objective still sustains restraint. Where a decisive position is worth more than cooperation it closes as $p_d\to1$, where this theory and structural realism agree, so the test discriminates at intermediate $p_d$ (Section 5.5) [num: JX].

**What would refute it.** Suppose a system moves every threshold of Table 7.1 to its safe side for every capable actor, by the marks that Section 8.2 lists. If the signatures then persist unchanged over the dwell time (Section 11.4), the claim fails. If moving one shared parameter changes only its own signature over that time, the claim that the conditions interlock fails for that parameter. Conversely, durable safety obtained by constraining conduct while the thresholds stay where they were, maintained as stakes and speed grow and classified as such before the outcome is known, would refute the claim that the conditions must change.

If the diagnosis holds, the question is what would change it. Part III compares paths by one standard, and takes two things from this section: Table 7.1, as the test of what a path changes, and the status column of Section 7.3, as the evidence that the range of uncertainty over which every path is judged must respect.

# Part III. Responses: what would change the trajectory

Part III's standard of comparison is a judgment of value, its descriptions of arrangements are definitions, and its formal results hold under the premises each states; which whole design to prefer is left to Part IV.

## 8. Comparing paths, and the alternatives on their strongest terms

> **Claim.** Paths are compared by one standard that protects irreversible losses first and applies one range of uncertainty to every path, each path's own failure modes included [val]. An arrangement is described by what it changes, not by its name: which thresholds of Table 7.1 it moves, to which side and for whom, and whether its enforcement survives the arrival of a decisive position [def]. An arrangement whose fallback is no more hazardous than continuation, and whose own hazards while it lasts, capture and collusion included, are no higher, survives at least as well as continuation at every horizon, however its dissolution is decided [thm]. Where every path's worst case crosses the tolerance, as it does under the rule for the range, the ranking falls to the standard's third step and is a judgment [val]. That arrangements which constrain conduct while leaving the thresholds where they were erode as stakes and speed grow, while those that move them for every capable actor persist, is a registered prediction [conj].
>
> **Why.** Labels such as "regulation", "cooperation" or "agreement" decide nothing: every arrangement that changes the map from conduct to share, resources and standing changes selection, and what decides is the direction, for whom, and whether the change survives success [def]. Met at their strongest, the alternatives that keep the contest leave thresholds where they were, each by a mechanism shown in its own model: a lead decays and is judged by its holder; a winner's restraint after victory rests on its need for the governed; a freeze after a sprint is a stop of the most unequal incidence, held by the largest conversion target; verification bounds defection and leaves the value of winning in place [thm∣prem].

### 8.1 The standard of comparison

**The protection standard [val].** Each candidate path is evaluated at common horizons, near (about five years), medium (about twenty) and long (fifty or more), over one justified range $\Theta$ of uncertain parameters applied to every path. First, bound the probability of O1 and O2 within each horizon over $\Theta$. Second, among paths whose bound stays below a stated tolerance, prefer the one with greater useful benefit, more fairly distributed, that keeps more options of correction open. Third, if no path meets the tolerance, prefer the one with the lowest bound that keeps options open. This is robust satisficing on irreversible losses, a normative choice, and the criteria it declines rank paths otherwise. An expected-value criterion can favour a small probability of a very large loss when the expected benefit is large. Under discounted expected utility the acceptable existential risk turns on how fast the value of a year of life rises with consumption: at a 1% annual hazard and 10% growth, a planner with logarithmic utility accepts about a one-in-three cumulative risk for a 55-fold gain in consumption, and with curvature 2 about 4% (Jones 2024) [obs: EV11]; if utility is linear in consumption, so that the value of life does not rise with it, a course is preferred whenever the hazard it adds is below the growth it adds, though it ends in catastrophe with certainty (S8) [thm]. O1 protects catastrophic, common-cause losses of life, and the standard does not discount them; deaths from benefits forgone are counted as a cost of the path, not as O1, and their weight is the normative crux N1, with the margin that only the decisive class could supply measured as crux C10.

**The range $\Theta$ [val].** A parameter value enters $\Theta$ if it is consistent with the dated evidence of the register and is produced by a stated mechanism whose premises are named, with a precedent or base rate where one exists. A value that needs a premise the register contradicts is excluded. The same rule applies to every path: a rising uncovered share on the continuation path enters, because its premises are named mechanisms the register does not contradict (EV5–EV7); and equally the capture of a common custodian, dissolution, coercive enforcement, the interruption of services and collusion among checks enter on the cooperative path, with the failure modes of every alternative of Section 8.3 (X8). Every entry of $\Theta$ cites its source.

**Definition 8.1 (The correctable kernel) [def].** *The correctable kernel is the set of states of the world from which some non-anticipative strategy of admissible correction avoids O1 and O2 against every sequence of errors in the justified range $\Theta$: a union over strategies of correction and an intersection over sequences of error. In viability theory it is a discriminating, or guaranteed-viability, kernel of a game against disturbances (Cardaliaguet 1996; Cardaliaguet, Quincampoix and Saint-Pierre 1999), not the viability kernel of undisturbed dynamics.*

Leaving the kernel is the loss of guaranteed correctability, a point of no guaranteed return earlier than the losses themselves. The corpus describes openness by four capacities, each a condition of the kernel [corpus: possibility-space]: *not yet exhausted* (未), no decisive end state reached; *still possible* (可), plurality of lineages, purposes and correctors kept; *still responsive* (应), humans able to suspend, modify or replace; *still able to turn back* (转), reversal possible. A closure made to protect openness, such as a defence, a quarantine or an emergency power, can state what would make it withdraw; a closure that cannot state that has left the kernel. The corpus names the two: 护闭, closing one place for a time to protect a larger opening, and 成闭, a closing that does not reopen [corpus: possibility-space]. The standard's second and third steps, which prefer what keeps options of correction open, are a preference for staying inside the kernel. This paper computes no kernel; what Theorem 10.4 computes is a boundary of viable parameters under one fixed strategy, a different and smaller question.

**Lemma 8.2 (When an arrangement cannot do worse) [thm].** Two design conditions guarantee that an arrangement does no worse than continuation at any horizon. *Let an arrangement $A$ and continuation $C$ be driven by the same exogenous world process, the irreversible event on each path occurring at date $t$ when a common uniform draw falls below that path's conditional hazard given the shared history. Suppose that on every history on which both paths have survived to $t$: (safe fallback) once $A$ has dissolved, its hazard is no higher than $C$'s; and (bounded own risk) while $A$ lasts, its hazard, its own hazards of capture, restriction and collusion included, is no higher than $C$'s. Then $A$'s survival probability is at least $C$'s at every horizon, whatever the rule by which $A$ dissolves, rules that depend on the state included.*

*Scope.* Where bounded own risk fails, there is no pointwise dominance and the standard compares the worst cases of both paths; that is where regulated competition can win. *Proof:* S8, which also shows that comparing after conditioning on the dissolution date fails when dissolution depends on the state [num: SM(4), SM(14), FD].

The two conditions are design conditions, read from an arrangement's own documents; the first is the durability test D1 of Definition 8.4. An agreement whose fallback is a short sprint from preserved relative standing violates the first; a design whose own concentration, capture or collusion can exceed continuation's hazards violates the second, and whether the design of Section 10 does is crux C4.

Comparing by survival does not by itself make restraint an equilibrium. Write $\Phi^R$, $\Phi^T$, $\Phi^P$ and $\Phi^S$ for an actor's probability of surviving to a common horizon when all restrain, when it races against restrainers, when all race, and when it restrains against a racer, each counting the hazards of that branch: delegation's loss of the capacity to overrule, the shared hazard, preventive attack, a rival's decisive win. With trust $\varpi$ that the other restrains, the advantage of restraint is $\varpi(\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(1-\varpi)(\Phi^S-\Phi^P)$, so the game in survival terms is dominance, a stag hunt, chicken or a prisoner's dilemma by the signs of the two differences; mutual racing stays a strict equilibrium at every horizon at which racing beside a racer survives better than staying out, $\Phi^P>\Phi^S$; and where the arrangement's own survival also decays, signs that hold at near and medium horizons can reverse at long ones (Proposition S8.1) [thm]. For a leader whose exit would hand a rival a decisive win, the shared hazard is what carries the case for restraint [num: legacy v4.4, SV].

So paths are compared at common finite horizons, not at an infinite one at which both branches of a hazardous world can tend to zero. The protection standard does not by itself rank continuation with strong correction against a design that changes selection: under the rule for $\Theta$ the worst case of each includes an irreversible loss, so both fail the tolerance there and the comparison falls to the standard's third step. Section 12.1 states the ranking at that step as a judgment, with its reasons, over the failure modes of every path that X8 lists.

### 8.2 Profiles, not labels

**Definition 8.3 (What an arrangement changes) [def].** *An arrangement is described by a profile: which mechanisms of Part I it neutralizes, weakens, leaves or strengthens, read as the thresholds of Table 7.1 it moves and the side to which it moves them; its coverage (unilateral, plurilateral, every capable actor); its bindingness (Abbott et al. 2000); whether its enforcement survives a decisive position, and if it prevents one from arriving, by what verified mechanism; whether its beneficiaries can amend it; its fallback and own risk; and its durability. And selection: every arrangement that changes the map from conduct to share, resources and standing changes selection, so what is recorded is the direction, whether it moves the thresholds toward their safe side or away from it, and whether in every capable actor's game or only in rivals'.*

Export controls aimed at preserving a lead change selection, toward a lead: blocking diffusion to rivals raises the structural loop gain in the channels it reaches, the gain the lead is meant to exploit (Section 2.2). Deterrence by threatened sabotage acts on $p_d$, as custody does, by a different means and with a different fallback.

**The generative direction.** A change of selection in the generative direction has seven marks:
- **G1** consequences return to their source;
- **G2** winning confers reward, not more advantage in the same game;
- **G3** success cannot buy control over its own assessment, and correction stays plural, decorrelated and in common hands;
- **G4** decisive capability is held in common or not built;
- **G5** provision of the conditions of a decent life sits beneath the game;
- **G6** measurement and language are shared;
- **G7** pace is bound to understanding.

Each reverses thresholds of Table 7.1, in the model of the row it reverses [thm, row by row]: G1 load, and the alignment tax where admission returns a divergence's common cost to its developer; G2 and G5 together compounding and standing, since non-convertible reward works only with provision not won in the contest, and G5 holds down the won share, the pivot of the scissors (Proposition 5.17); G3 capture; G4 enforcement, by keeping $p_d$ small for every deviation (Corollary 5.7); G6 talk; G7 coverage and tempo, the capability bound for masked classes, and, applied to decisions, delegation, which narrow E1 keeps within the pace of human correction. G2 is the author's rule of game design (Section 2.2), and the seven are what Section 3.7 asks of a selection that counts the system. What this paper argues for is a change of selection with these marks, covering every capable actor, enforceable and durable, whatever it is called.

**Definition 8.4 (Durability tests) [def].** *An arrangement is tested on its own documents and estimates, not on assumptions added by the tester: D1, its fallback if it dissolves; D2, its estimated hazard of breakdown and what drives it; D3, who keeps informed veto, modification, replacement and exit after any delegation; D4, whether its own correctors have full inbound row sums below one; D5, what happens when its strongest participant violates it; D6, succession and the continuity of essential services; D7, common causes and collusion among its checks and systems; D8, whether membership is open or stops at a club.*

The tests apply to every path, the cooperative design of Section 10 included, and by one evidence standard: each entry is read from the arrangement's own documents and estimates, and where those give none, the entry says so (X8). An arrangement with the form of cooperation that fails D1–D3 on its own estimates may be called a *pseudo-solution*; the label summarizes test results and does not replace them. **Registered prediction [conj]:** arrangements that constrain conduct while leaving the thresholds of Table 7.1 where they were erode as stakes and speed grow, because the mechanisms of Sections 3 to 5 keep acting on them; those that move the thresholds to their safe side for every capable actor persist, because what would erode them has been removed. The classification uses the profile, never the outcome, and Section 7.4 states what would refute it.

### 8.3 The alternatives

Each alternative is met in its strongest form, on its merits rather than its prominence, and answered by mechanism: what remains, which thresholds it leaves, and what would show that it suffices. X8 gives each in full, with how it would be installed.

**(a) Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction.** *Strongest form:* control scoped to early transformative systems and used under control to accelerate alignment and oversight while the window lasts (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024); weak supervisors eliciting part of a stronger model's capability (Burns et al. 2023); auditors with access to training data finding a planted objective that black-box access missed (Marks et al. 2025); oversight with the right topology (Proposition 6.9); AI-assisted correction keeping pace with cheapening control (Proposition 4.5); research that drives the alignment tax to zero or below, class by class, so that the safe variant is also the winning one and its safeguards spread without coordination (Section 6.4); and, in its proponents' own plans, a strong international agreement ranked first. *What remains:* principal alignment does not change the game among principals (Proposition 6.1); the window lasts only as long as the race allows; systems are retained for performance (Lemma 6.5), and progress that lowers the hazard per unit of racing or delegation is partly absorbed by more of both (Proposition S6.2); redundant oversight is capped by the common causes selection produces, and certified row sums are a sufficient certificate, not a proof of independent errors (Proposition 6.9); AI-assisted correction is only as independent as its funding, access, appointment and lineage; time is a part of the tax that no technique removes; for masked divergences admission removes only what it detects, so capability has to be bounded in common (Remark 6.8); and which divergences are tolerated is set at the outermost loop (Proposition 6.7). *Profile:* it leaves compounding, standing and enforcement where they were, and changes selection only when held by correctors separated from success, under constraints common to all; it is then part of G3 and G7.

**(b) Regulated competition with strong correction.** *Strongest form:* liability and licensing make consequences return; evaluation access is mandated and thresholds are fixed in advance; where the effective hazard is high and capability transparent, pacing can be the unique equilibrium of a capability race without any agreement (Fudenberg and Koh 2026); civil nuclear power after a major accident, and aviation with independent investigation and insurance pricing, show durable safety with unchanged commercial motives [obs]. *What remains:* coverage across jurisdictions, since the race is set by the most race-inclined actor (Proposition 5.1); the rule-makers are themselves in a contest of standing (Proposition 9.2); liability cannot exceed the assets of the liable (Shavell 1986), so a catastrophic or common harm cannot be turned wholly into a private cost; two developers' frameworks condition commitments on rivals, and one unreviewed study codes 77% of the changes it traced, in twelve pairs of consecutive framework versions, as weakening [obs: EV2]; and the precedents had no decisive prize. Pacing needs a hazard that the racer itself bears and transparency, which G1 and G6 secure and the conditions of winning erode: relative accounting weighs a shared hazard by $1+\theta$, and not at all at $\theta=-1$ (Proposition 3.5), and quiet deviations, captured evaluation and masked divergences defeat transparency (Proposition 5.4, Proposition 4.8, Remark 6.8; X8). *Profile:* it depends on content. Regulation that returns consequences and separates evaluators from success, across every capable actor, is G1 and G3 for all, and the change this paper argues for, whatever it is called; regulation that constrains conduct while success can still buy the rule-makers is predicted to erode as stakes and speed grow.

**(c) Leadership and a safety margin.** *Strongest form:* a leader spends part of its lead on safety; a large lead can discourage a follower (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009); and a developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains, because rivals slow too (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026), a local result that needs no lead. *What remains:* a lead shrinks by diffusion even when the follower does not accelerate, a six-month lead diffusing at a tenth a month lasting under five months [num: legacy v4.5, LD]; spending it gives up the discouragement the strongest form relies on and returns the contest toward parity, where effort is greatest and, in contests in which a lead discourages the follower, standing is priced highest (Section 7.2); what the margin must buy is coverage, and for hazard classes whose evidence lag exceeds it, it buys nothing (Proposition 5.12); whether it suffices is judged inside the contest, by the leader's own evaluators under time pressure; and a leader that sees its window closing has a motive to strike first (Remark S8.2). *Profile:* it acts on tempo for a time and leaves the objective of winning in place. Its best case, a first commitment to slow that rivals answer by slowing, is a first step of the transition (Section 11), not a substitute for it.

**(d) The benign winner: win, bind, share.** *Strongest form:* a coalition seeks a clear advantage, including military superiority and the denial of key inputs to adversaries, and then offers its benefits to others in exchange for their support (EV1); victors have bound themselves through institutions (Ikenberry 2001), a dominant power can supply public goods (Kindleberger 1973), and an encompassing ruler internalizes the shared conditions (Olson 1993). It persuades by promising both security and benefits; both rest on the winner's restraint after the fact. By this paper's own results the form is strongest where the winner still needs the governed: under logarithmic utility a winner accurate toward what can be divided gives them $\kappa_W/(1+\kappa_W)$ (Section 5.2). *What remains:* after a decisive win what the others receive is set by the winner's objective alone, and a promise to share is worth breaking, with no one to sanction it (Proposition 5.8) [thm]; the winner's need for the governed after victory is what the race cannot verify beforehand, and what AI can lower (C13); where conduct before victory and after it go together, the race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance (Proposition 7.3) [thm∣prem]; and the winner is itself a field of agencies, firms, laboratories and systems, whose restraint after victory depends on whether its own correctors can be bought by its successes. *Profile:* it changes nothing in selection before victory. What would show it suffices are the commitment tests: binding limits on what victory confers, benefits shared as they arise rather than promised, and the coalition's own correctors certified independent, with succession rules that survive changes of leadership (Test I). A winner that adopts these has changed what it selects for.

**(e) A finite sprint, then restraint.** *Strongest form:* sprint to a decisive lead, then freeze or roll back and govern at the tempo of understanding. A winner that freezes keeps positive survival and gives up only permanent superiority (Proposition 5.14), and after a decisive win no contest remains to price standing. *What remains*, by kind of objection. Exposure: the path passes through its greatest exposure, and through the rivals' strongest motive for preventive attack (Proposition 5.9), before any restraint begins; whether that exposure is survivable is C1 and C5. Maintenance: a decisive advantage not used to stop others' growth does not stay decisive, and using it so ends their O2, which V2 and V3 protect (Remark S8.3). Mechanism, the paper's own answer: the freeze is a stop that one actor imposes on all the others, the most unequal incidence a constraint can have, so it re-sorts those it binds and is evaded wherever evasion pays their type (Lemma 3.3(2), (3)) [thm], and they read it as a lead being kept; and it is held by the largest conversion target there is, since the return to influence over correctors grows with the square of share (Proposition 4.3; Proposition 4.5(4)) [thm]. The stop is not what separates the sprint from E2 and common custody, which also stop every actor from building the decisive class; incidence and holder are. The sprint's freeze answers to the sprinter's own correctors and, when the sprinter breaks it, to no one (D4, D5); custody is designed for separated functions under correctors certified independent of every party, certificates not yet measured (C4; X8 sets the two side by side). *Profile:* nothing changes in selection during the sprint.

**(f) Deterrence and prevention.** Prevention can be survival-preferred by the preventer.

**Proposition 8.5 (Prevention under the preventer's own criterion) [thm].** *Let an actor compare courses by its own survival at a common horizon, facing a rival it believes certain to race. (1) It strictly prefers a single strike, which escalates to catastrophe with probability $\varepsilon_P$ and otherwise succeeds with probability $s_P$ in returning the field to something like the arrangement, leaving the race as it was if it fails, to both staying out and joining iff $\varepsilon_P<1-\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/\big(s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big)$; as the racing branches $\Phi^S$ and $\Phi^P$ deteriorate, this bound rises toward one wherever $s_P\Phi^R>0$. (2) Sustained prevention against a target that rebuilds with probability at least $b_P$ a period, each strike escalating with probability at least $\varepsilon_{\min}$, has a per-period hazard of at least $b_P\varepsilon_{\min}$.*

*Scope.* Prevention that permanently ends the target's capacity, or converts into joint monitoring, is a different branch. *Proof:* S8, which also compares sustained prevention with racing at common horizons [num: legacy v5, FS].

The objections are three, and none changes the criterion. Normatively, prevention ends or endangers others' O1 and O2 (V2, V3). Epistemically, aimed at a rival believed certain to race, it can make a rival that was in fact survival-first act as the type it was feared to be (Jervis 1976; Kydd 1997). Technically, detecting unobservable progress and striking at machine speed brings the delegation of Section 6.3. Deterrence by threatened sabotage acts on $p_d$ by threat, observing datacentres and making compute thresholds salient (Hendrycks, Schmidt and Wang 2025); its fallback is war or race, its credibility can reverse under survival-first comparisons, its red lines are hard to state and to monitor (X8), and algorithmic progress erodes thresholds stated in compute [obs: EV9].

Insuring against falling behind is not an exit either: the penalty is set by the winner, not the laggard, and insurance accepts the frame of the contest, hedging against losing instead of declining the game (Remark S8.4).
