That is the case the ruling of Section 3.8 closes: against a winner of that logic falling behind cannot be made survivable, so a remedy has to oppose the logic, not hedge inside it. Provision differs by what it is for. Held by no one, beneath the game, it lowers the won share for every actor (G5); held by one actor as insurance against losing, it is a position in the contest, which a winner with a relative objective has a motive to destroy.

**The hard case.** A survival-first actor that gives a low probability to a capable rival's holding an unbounded relative objective (Section 3.8) has a best course: to propose verifiable limits and to take narrow E1 verifiably in its own strategic loops, a costly signal (Proposition 11.2). With a high probability it compares denial, a coalition that stops the decisive class, and prevention, and coverage of every capable actor then comes from consent or from coercion. Coercive denial raises the weight on winning and the trust threshold and, if it escalates, falls under Proposition 8.5. The theory does not derive that its net effect is positive and states the case as hard; Section 12.1 states, once, the conditions under which a coercive restriction of frontier inputs enters even the comparison of designs.

**(g) A verified agreement over a contest that remains.** Rivals agree to slow and to delay decisive capability, with compute declarations, supply-chain accounting, recomputation of workloads and mutually assured destruction of compute if the agreement dissolves. Let the payoffs of mutual restraint, of racing against a restrainer, of mutual racing and of restraining against a racer be $R$, $T$, $P$ and $S$; let verification detect a defection in time with probability $\upsilon$, in which case it is reversed, the defector receiving $P-F$ and the cooperator $P$; let trust $\varpi$ be the belief that the other cooperates; and write $g_C=R-\upsilon(P-F)-(1-\upsilon)T$ for what cooperating gains against a cooperator and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$ for what it loses against a defector.

**Proposition 8.6 (Assurance) [thm].** *In this symmetric two-player game: (1) cooperation is a best response iff $\varpi\ge g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ when both are positive; no trust suffices if $g_C\le0<g_D$; cooperation is dominant if $g_D\le0<g_C$. (2) Where $T-P+F>0$ and the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating, $T>R$, cooperating against a cooperator pays iff $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$; where $T<R$, it pays without verification.*

*Scope.* The threshold of (2) is the deterrence condition of the inspection game (Avenhaus, von Stengel and Zamir 2002). Read with its payoffs as continuation values, it is the enforcement condition seen from verification, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d}$ (Remark 5.5): timely detection must exceed a level that rises with the value of winning. *Proof:* S8, with the $n$-player bounds and the condition under which they fail.

**Theorem 8.7 (Verification and the value of winning) [thm].** Verification is required exactly when, and to the degree that, the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating.
1. *For a fixed sanction, the timely detection that cooperation needs, $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, tends to one as $T$ grows.*
2. *Detection counts only if it comes before a defection becomes decisive, so that $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$; acceleration, which shortens $T_{\rm dec}$, lowers the timely detection that any given instrument achieves.*
3. *Verification can also lower $T$ by bounding the largest defection that would go undetected, for instance by accounting for compute, which does not need near-certain detection. The bound holds while the verified coalition's advantage in effective compute stays above the ratio at which an undetected project would become decisive [def]; since effective compute is algorithmic efficiency times compute, the coalition's compute advantage must then grow at least as fast as diffusion erodes its algorithmic one. That it does, the premise (advantage keeps pace), is not derived here [conj], and the rate of erosion is observed (EV9).*

*Scope.* Verification does not carry unverifiable information (Proposition 5.16), its own reliability (Section 4), or future commitments, unless the sanction keeps pace with the net gain from exit, which grows with the capability held in check when leaving with a decisive advantage gains faster than staying with one's share; an agreement that preserves relative positions preserves the relative term of every payoff, and with it the motive to exit (Proposition S8.5) [thm]. *Proof:* S8.

One detailed plan of this kind, among leading powers, is designed to be enforceable without trust, with proportional reserves and a coalition that must stay ahead of covert projects (EV10; X8). It stands for a position, keep the contest, slow it and stay safe, and the answer above is to the position, not to the plan's figures. *Profile:* it slows and caps, acting on tempo and decisiveness, and keeps relative standing in its enforcement and fallback, and with it the motive to exit. That makes it a bridge. Whether it can be the end depends on whether it lowers the value of winning, as G2 and G5 do, or only bounds defection, as (3) of Theorem 8.7 does.

### 8.4 The comparison

| Path | What it changes | Thresholds of Table 7.1 it leaves on the danger side | Fallback (D1) | Main residual risk | Crux |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction alone | Residual harm per unit; tempo; the alignment tax, where tax-lowering results are published | Compounding; standing; enforcement; the alignment tax for masked classes | The race | The game among principals; absorption; common causes; the outer loop | C1, C3, C9, C12 |
| Regulated competition within jurisdictions | Load and capture (G1, G3), within one jurisdiction | Enforcement and compounding across jurisdictions; capture of the rule-makers in their own contest | The race | Coverage across jurisdictions; the rule-makers' contest; liability's ceiling | C2, EV2 |
| Leadership and margin | Tempo, for a time | Compounding; standing, highest at the parity to which a spent lead returns; enforcement | The race, at parity | The margin decays and is judged inside | C5 |
| The benign winner | Nothing before victory | Enforcement, replaced after victory by the winner's objective; capture inside the winner | Domination | Others' O2 rests on restraint no one can verify beforehand ($\kappa_W$); the winner's internal field | C4, C13, Test I |
| Finite sprint, then restraint | Tempo, after the sprint | During the sprint, enforcement and coverage; after it, load, through the most unequal incidence, and capture, through the largest conversion target | Prevention | Peak exposure and prevention during the sprint; the sprinter as its own corrector | C1, C5 |
| Deterrence by sabotage | $p_d$, by threat | Compounding; standing; delegation, which militarization pushes toward danger | War or race | Misjudgment; militarization; unclear red lines | C7 |
| Coercive restriction of frontier inputs (compared in Section 12.1 under its own conditions) | $p_d$, by denial; the weight on winning, raised | Load, through unequal incidence unless the restrictor bears it too; capture, the restrictor being the largest conversion target | Escalation (Proposition 8.5) | Escalation; the restrictor's correctors | C2, C7 |
| Verified agreement over a remaining contest | Tempo and decisiveness, bounded | Standing and compounding, with relative positions preserved | A short sprint from preserved standing | Fallback and handoff; an overhang that raises the gain from exit | C5, C7 |
| A design changing selection (Section 10) | The shared parameters, for every capable actor, by G1–G7 | None by design; whether it holds them is C4, and its own hazards enter $\Theta$ | Separately held functions (Section 10.5) | Its own capture, dissolution and restriction | C4, C9 |

The comparison turns on two quantities. If continuation keeps the uncovered hazardous share summable, it can beat a fragile cooperative arrangement; if the uncovered share rises on the continuation path, any cooperative arrangement with a modest hazard of dissolution does better at every horizon past the near term [num: legacy v5, CMP; S8]. The first is crux C1; the second is what Sections 10 and 11 design for and test. At the standard's third step (Section 8.1) the comparison needs estimates on both sides, and those estimates are what the cruxes of Section 12.3 would supply. X8 lists, path by path, each failure mode in $\Theta$, whether and into what it can be reversed, whether that is derived or only designed, and what would show it under way: the base on which Section 12.1 compares. An expected-value criterion that puts low probability on a rising uncovered share and values speed highly can prefer continuation, and a discounted criterion with utility linear in consumption prefers racing whenever the hazard it adds is below the growth it adds. These disagreements are the normative cruxes. The analysis does not establish that every cooperative arrangement outperforms regulated competition, and Section 12 does not claim it.

Section 9 states what any adequate arrangement must do to move the thresholds for every capable actor, whatever it is called, taking from here the profile and the marks G1–G7; Section 12 takes the durability tests, the failure modes of X8 and the third step of the standard.

## 9. What any adequate arrangement must do

> **Claim.** What every adequate arrangement must do can be stated before designs are compared: each requirement with its kind of support, necessary in a named model over a named horizon, sufficient, or normative, and with the conditions under which it does not bind [def]. Where (reach) holds with a race carrier present, and no single rule-maker reaches every carrier whose load can carry the host below its floor, several requirements cannot be met by any actor alone, and competition among actors does not supply them [thm].
>
> **Why.** A requirement stated with its model and its exceptions can be checked against any design, regulated competition included, which can then be found to meet it [def]. A constraint that one carrier adopts alone is a cost borne by it alone, which relative selection removes while effective liability is below the private advantage; a rule-maker that does not reach every such carrier leaves the host to those it does not reach; and rule-makers in a contest of standing, weighing it above a stated level, do not impose a liability alone [thm]. Every sanction, observation and count of coverage that the requirements call for is a corrector's act [def]: where success buys the rule-makers, an enacted liability binds only in the part of rule-making not bought [thm], and the other requirements weaken through the interfaces that Table 7.1 marks [thm∣prem].

Part I proved its results one model at a time, and Table 7.1 reads them as thresholds, each with a side on which a protected outcome is lost. Read in reverse, such a result is a requirement: an arrangement that does not keep the field on the safe side loses the outcome, in that model and over that horizon. The kind of support decides how a row may be used. A necessary row is a test: a design that lacks it fails in the row's model within its horizon. A sufficient row is a route, not a test. A normative row rests on a value of Section 1.3 and is declared, not derived. The means by which a design meets a row are not rows; they are the subject of Section 10. Read together, the rows state with their models the third job of "we are one" (Section 3.7), a selection that counts the system. S9 derives each row, fixes its scope with counterexamples, and maps the rows onto the paper's other lists (Table S9.2).

**Table 9.1 (Requirements, by the kind of support each has) [def, with support: each row carries the tag of the result it cites].** *Each row holds only in the model and over the horizon of its third column. The last column names the routes by which an arrangement does without it, and marks what meets a row rather than excusing it. The table organizes results proved elsewhere and ranks no design.*

| Requirement | Kind | Needed for, in which model | Basis | Not needed when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **R1** Consequences return: long-run effective liability at least the private advantage of load, $\limsup_t\frac1t\int_0^t\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu$; sharper, a cumulative advantage of load, $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$, that does not tend to infinity | necessary [thm] | viability at every horizon; the host (3), payoff-monotone selection among carriers otherwise alike, a race carrier present, (reach) | Proposition 3.4; Theorem 10.4(3) | a horizon that ends before the floor is reached; (reach) fails; other advantages correlated with restraint that offset the load (the scope of Proposition 3.4) |
| **R2** Coverage: the common constraints reach every actor whose share can grow | necessary [thm] | the same, with a carrier the constraints do not reach; a race that begins if any one capable actor prefers it (Proposition 5.1) | Proposition 3.4; Proposition 5.1; Lemma 3.3(2) | harms that stay local; uncovered carriers whose combined share is bounded so that their load cannot carry the host below its floor; a horizon shorter than the crossing time |
| **R3a** Independent correction certified at the start and growing at least at $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$, faster by $w\delta_h$ where it rests on human capacity that delegation erodes | sufficient at any speed of adaptation [thm]; necessary where capacity tracks its selected level, $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$ [thm] | the evaluation certificate at every date; the field model with a seeded evaluation domain, a positive cultivation channel, $[H_\gamma]$ and no per-unit cost floor | Proposition 4.5(2); Proposition 4.6(1) | $\chi=0$ or the conversion channels removed, where a level of correction suffices (Proposition 4.5(1)); a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower, where the requirement is again a level (Proposition 4.5(3)); a floor that AI lowers more slowly than correction grows, for the certificate from some date on; capacity selected gradually, its variance not rising faster than capacity, for the certificate in the end (Proposition S4.2); nothing captured; a horizon shorter than the failure time |
| **R3b** No core of mutually sustaining capture among the corrector domains | necessary [thm] | no capture from every initial state; the field model | Proposition 4.10(4) | only the states actually reached need be excluded |
| **R4** Breakouts within the enforceable bound: $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for every actor $i$ and every deviation $a$ with $D_i(a)>v_i$; where punishment is triggered by detection, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ | necessary [thm] | restraint enforced by repetition under perfect monitoring, where a deviation can reach a decisive position worth more than the punishment | Proposition 5.4(2); Corollary 5.7; Remark 5.5 | no deviation reaches a position beyond punishment, so that $p_d=0$ and ordinary repetition decides (Branch III; Branch I′ above its saturating level; Proposition 7.2); $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$ for every actor and deviation, that is $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$, which a decisive position worth less than cooperation does not by itself give (Corollary 5.7). Detection and response faster than breakout meet R4; they do not excuse it |
| **R5** A summable uncovered hazardous share in each hazard class, $\sum_tu_t<\infty$ with $u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ | necessary under (residual floor) [thm∣prem]; sufficient where residual risk is at most $\bar hu_t$ with $\bar h<1$ [thm] | survival not tending to zero as the horizon grows; every actor's under (shared failure), whoever is racing | Lemma 5.11; Theorem 5.13 | (residual floor) fails, as where a correction architecture whose blocking becomes complete makes residual risk summable without coverage (Proposition S6.4); a finite horizon, where what counts is the exposure up to it (Proposition 5.14). Where hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need while coverage keeps growing, R5 is met, not excused (Proposition 5.15) |
| **R6** Observation independent of the observed, with any bound on masking drawn from evidence the observed does not control; shared measurement; verifiable signals | sufficient [thm] | alarms that fire no later than the condition they watch reaches its target; trust without good will | Remark 4.9; Proposition 4.8; Proposition 5.16 | channels that observe actions directly, such as verifiable deployments, compute and the performance of contracts, for what they observe; for trust, stakes on the topic of talk that are not contested |
| **R7** Standing for those affected; inclusion that does not stop at a club | normative [val] | V3 | Proposition 3.12 shows harm to outsiders over-produced, not that O1 or O2 is reached | never, for the normative part |
| **R8a** Common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, for divergences whose costs fall on common terms, with an expected sanction at least the gain from evasion for every type that can evade; decisions retained by contest performance only inside them | necessary [thm∣prem]; the sanction condition necessary within a type and sufficient across types [thm] | relative, cumulative selection among developers under $(A_\tau)$, a matched variant admissible; decisions retained by contest performance | Proposition 6.7(3); Lemma 6.5; Lemma 3.3(3) | developers selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate that does not merely move the selection to the level of states; a tax zero or negative for the class, so that the safeguard spreads without coordination (C9), including a tax made negative by a liability that returns the common cost to the developer where harm can be attributed, the route of R1; decisions not retained by contest performance |
| **R8b** Equal incidence of the common constraints among those who comply | necessary and sufficient for neutrality among compliers [thm]; required by V5 [val] | a rule that neither picks winners among those who comply nor bars their challengers; payoff-monotone selection, costs additive | Lemma 3.3(2); Definition 4.1 | not needed to keep a divergence out: unequal incidence re-sorts compliers without letting it back in (S9) |
| **R9** Human decision authority kept: where delegation would erode the capacity to take consequential decisions back, that capacity kept above its floor in practice, or the decisions not delegated past the lock | necessary [thm∣prem] | O2 in each such domain; capacity that decays under delegation and recovers slowly without it, in a contest that denies the time to recover | Lemma 6.3; Proposition 6.4 | delegation that leaves the capacity intact; automation bounded and reversible within the time available, as the test of O2 decides (Section 6.6). Where every actor prices in a lock it knows to be certain, R9 is met without an arrangement (Proposition 6.4(1)) |

**Reading the rows.** Two senses of coverage are kept apart: R2's is of actors, the reach of the common constraints; R5's is of hazard modes, the share of hazardous variety under correction that meets the four conditions of Section 5.3. R8 is split because its clauses do different work (Section 3.2): the sanction keeps a constraint, and equal incidence keeps it from becoming a weapon against challengers, the second direction of capture (Definition 4.1). R9 is the row for O2: the capacity to take decisions back survives delegation only where it is kept in practice, and keeping it costs strength where the contest is decided by strength (Lemma 6.3).

**The row the others rest on.** Every row but R7 rests on the work of correctors [def]. A liability binds at $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, what captured rule-making leaves of it (Section 4.1); the sanctions that bound a breakout and keep an admission constraint are applied by correctors, and are themselves subject to capture (Proposition 6.7(3)); detection, and the verification that capacity is kept in practice, are theirs; and assessment that success has captured does not count toward coverage. A design that meets the other rows with correctors that success can buy therefore meets R1 on paper only, since effective liability falls to zero as rule-making is captured [thm], and loses the others through the interfaces that Table 7.1 marks † [thm∣prem]. R3, correction that success cannot buy, is the row on which the table stands: the closure of Definition 2.4, seen from the side of what must be done.

**What the table does not list.** Sharing, provision beneath the game and reward that does not convert into control are not rows, because the models give them as alternatives, not necessities: equal standing is stable under sharing, lower decisiveness, provision not won in the contest or diffusion, any one of which can carry the margin (Proposition 2.3) [thm]. What sets them apart is that each meets several rows at once. Provision that grows with the stakes holds the won share still, and with it both sides of R4 (Proposition 5.17); reward that does not convert into control, $\chi=0$, brings R3a's requirement down to a level without second-order selection (Proposition 4.5(1)) [thm]. They are the levers of Section 10.2, and two of the generative marks of Section 8.2 name them.

**Where admission does not reach.** Admission reaches only what it admits, and three things lie beyond it. None makes R8a unnecessary, which is why they stand here and not in the last column; a design meets each by other means. Systems that automate a developer's own research are used first inside it, where a condition imposed at release cannot see them (Section 6.4); R8a is therefore stated for consequential deployment, internal deployment included. Openly released weights can be fine-tuned around any constraint (Section 6.4), so for an openly released class the decision to release is the admission decision for every later use, and what remains afterwards is a liability on use where harm can be attributed, the route of R1. And a masked divergence shows under evaluation only as far as the system's behaviour lets it, so admission conditioned on evaluated behaviour keeps it out only while the detection it allows, times the sanction, exceeds the tax (Remark 6.8). For masked classes R8a is necessary and not sufficient: admission is stated in capability, up to the level at which control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation remain safe, and beyond it the class falls under R5, stopped (E2) or held to the pace at which control covers it (E3). Whether admission can bound masking at the admitted capability is crux C12.

**Sufficient safeguards, and form.** Four safeguards are sufficient and not necessary. Full inbound row sums below one certify each corrector domain against every state of the others (Proposition 4.10(2)); they say nothing about whether the correctors' errors are independent (Proposition 6.9). The safeguards (I1)–(I4) of Section 10.3 give viability once in force, with a liability margin set by the host, the floor, the initial composition and the speed of selection; on a host that degrades by itself below a threshold, a shortfall in that margin is permanent (Theorem 10.4). Not building the decisive class, holding it in common custody, and detecting and answering faster than breakout meet R4 by changing the premise of Proposition 5.4 rather than its terms (Corollary 5.7(a)). And verification whose expected sanction is at least the net gain from exit deters irreversible exit from an agreement whose overhang grows (Proposition S8.5). The requirements constrain functions, not institutional form: one institution can meet them, and so can mutually recognized institutions of several jurisdictions, a federation or a polycentric complex [def]. Regulated competition meets the rows its rules carry, where those rules reach every capable actor and its correctors are separated from success [def]; what a single rule-maker cannot do is the next result.

**Proposition 9.2 (No unilateral solution) [thm].** Correction and coverage are not supplied by any one carrier, or by any one rule-maker in a contest of standing, and enacting a liability is not enough while rule-making is captured. *Let selection among carriers be payoff-monotone, with other advantages equal and a race carrier present.*
1. *While effective liability is below the private advantage, $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$, a carrier's unilateral contribution to the safeguards, whether lower load, correction keyed to no one or the cost of verification, lowers its payoff relative to otherwise identical carriers and is selected against.*
2. *Let no single rule-maker reach every carrier whose combined load satisfies (reach). Then no rule-maker alone meets R1 or R2, whatever it imposes. Let the $n_s$ rule-makers be in a contest of standing in which imposing the liability on the carriers one reaches is restraint in the commons of Proposition 3.7, worth $\Upsilon_s>1$ to the field per unit of its private cost. If each weighs winning by $\lambda_s>(\Upsilon_s-n_s)/(n_s-1)$, no rule-maker imposes the liability alone in equilibrium; whether they impose it jointly is governed by Proposition 8.6.*
3. *Where captured rule-making leaves effective liability $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, load has a negative private payoff, and selection lowers it, only once $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})>\nu$: enacting $\tau>\nu$ is not enough while $x_{\rm rule}\ge1-\nu/\tau$.*

*Proof:* S9.

**Which rows need more than one party.** R2 does by definition, and so does the shared measurement of R6. R1 does by parts (1) and (2), and part (3) ties it to R3, a second-order public good that competitors do not supply (Proposition 4.4). Of the means of R4, custody needs more than one party, not building binds only the one that refrains, and detection faster than breakout needs access to the conduct of others. Under (shared failure) each actor's survival turns on the uncovered share of all, so a pace kept by one leaves R5 unmet for every actor (Theorem 5.13). Under $(A_\tau)$ an admission constraint adopted by one developer is selected against (Proposition 6.7(3)). And where the contest is decided by strength, capacity kept in practice costs strength, so R9 kept by one is kept at a loss (Lemma 6.3). Where the mechanisms are active, a minimum of cooperation is therefore necessary: binding commitments covering every capable actor, with a layer of correction that no competitor controls [thm∣prem]. This is a result conditional on its models, combined with evidence and a stated standard, not a proof from first principles. Whether the present branch is one on which the mechanisms are active is Part II's diagnosis, and how much cooperation is warranted now is the judgment of Section 12 [val].

**Coverage can unravel.** A common constraint protects only while it covers every capable actor, and coverage is itself an equilibrium that can tip. A participant that leaves becomes a carrier the constraint does not reach (R2), and against it the constraint borne by those who remain is a cost borne by some, which relative selection removes (Lemma 3.3(2)). Where the cost of standing outside falls as more stand outside, because sanctions are funded by participants, shared provision is worth less with fewer inside, or rule-making is captured by those outside, staying and leaving are both stable, separated by a tipping share, as compliance and evasion are in Lemma 3.3(3) (Remark S9.1) [thm∣prem]. One large exit can carry the outside share past it, and the exits that follow need no further shock (compare minimum participation in Barrett 2003). A readiness rule that demands every dimension at once under noisy measurement errs toward holding a pause longer than its evidence warrants, which raises the cost of staying (Section 11.3) [conj]. Coverage can also fall without an exit, when the largest participant is exempted (EV18, read in X10) [obs], so the threshold is stated in coverage weighted by share, not in numbers of parties. An arrangement therefore states its participation threshold, type by type, and the conditions of access that keep the cost of standing outside above the cost of staying however many stand outside. A design that cannot state its threshold has not shown that it can last.

Section 10 takes the table function by function: what form each row needs, and how an order that meets the rows persists through what all share rather than through its winners.

## 10. The generative alternative

> **Claim.** The reflexive structure that lets success buy control over future selection can instead make contribution fund the conditions of further contribution [def]. The way out of an optimization trap is then neither a better optimization of the same objective nor the abolition of selection, competition or growth, but a change in what winning confers, so that the loop strengthens the shared host and independent correction instead of consuming them [thm∣prem]. Sharing and diffusion lower the structural gain in every channel; provision not won in the contest lowers it through capacity and, growing with the stakes, holds still the won share on which the scissors turn; correction lowers it through rule support, and success that cannot buy control over its own assessment leaves the reflexive channels no gain [thm]. None of these makes the selected intensity zero while a positional prize remains [thm], so that part has to be kept from forming or held in common [rec]. Where carriers differ only in load and one of low enough load is present, four safeguards in force before capture return consequences to their source, and the host stays above its floor throughout iff liability exceeds the private advantage by a margin the host sets [thm]. Where coverage is complete and correction is financed by formula, the generative order persists through what all share, not through its winners [thm∣prem], and its form follows function, not the axes of centralization or of individual and collective [def]. Whether it attracts is a conjecture [conj].
>
> **Why.** A term common to all is invisible to relative selection, so an order whose support no carrier can claim is not selected among carriers; it persists as a common constraint does, by coverage and by sanctions that leave no type a gain from evasion, with correctors financed by formula because no competitor supplies them [thm∣prem]. Each counter-mechanism of Part I requires its own form: one rule for all where coverage is the condition, many hands where independence is, common custody where a decisive position would otherwise form [def]. Every remedy for the selection of selectors is itself a selector, so no better guardian is an answer; a structure in which errors cannot grow without limit is [val].

### 10.1 The purpose

Taken out of the contest, capability stops being spent on standing. What the race dissipates, a share of the stakes that rises with decisiveness, with the shared loss and with the narrowness of the working objective (Proposition 5.2(1)), becomes available for need: medicine and public health, education, science, competent administration, infrastructure, and the long-horizon work that fragmented incentives under-supply because its value is common (Proposition S10.1). Cooperation is therefore not only a constraint on danger; its purpose is what AI can do for people when it is not the instrument by which one actor wins the common future. The joint value is large; the race consumes it, and a change in what is selected realizes it (V1).

**Authority as a productive capacity (V4).** Authority can coordinate investment, guarantee services, support learning and act on shared purposes. The ideal the theory draws on justifies authority by its responsibility for improving people's lives and the historical trajectory. It enters the design as a purpose to be sustained, not as a premise that it is realized: how institutions select and form officeholders, reward service, hear unwelcome evidence and revise harmful practices are causal variables, and a stated mission is not proof of its implementation. A larger causal radius brings larger responsibility, not larger entitlement; the author's book states the principle as "where power reaches, responsibility follows" (力之所及，责之所在) [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十八章]. With the norm of a shared future (Section 3.7), it is the institutional consequence of the relational self.

**Provision that does not become the system.** Taken out of the contest, AI can be what infrastructure is: provision of the conditions of a decent life, built to meet need rather than to gain standing. Trees are the image, in the author's words "the lungs of the ecosystem": they provide shelter and fruit, ask no return for it, and do not "become the whole ecosystem" [corpus: shengsheng]. The image carries the two conditions of the design: provision that asks no return keeps no gate, and provision that does not become the whole does not close. Part I supplies four properties. Such provision is *distributed*, many providers below the size at which influence over correctors pays (Proposition 4.3); *good enough*, stopping growth once a need is met, which keeps its hazard modes within correction and confers no decisive advantage (Proposition 5.15); *replaceable*, so that human capacity stays above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3); and *for everyone*, understanding and provision alike, not for a class of guardians (Section 10.4). Contribution is rewarded with revenue, contracts and recognition; what success does not buy is the right to appoint evaluators, set thresholds, exclude entrants or raise their cost of entry, or amend the governing rules (V5). That cuts the conversion of success into rule support (Section 4) and keeps the incentive to contribute. What is open is who builds and funds such provision, not who holds it: provision that does not become the whole is held by no one. Section 10.5 and Discussion X10 develop one answer to the open part, provision financed by formula and built by many providers under a cap on any one's share, as a candidate and not as the conclusion.

### 10.2 What winning confers: sharing, convertibility and provision beneath the game

The prize of the contest has two parts (Section 3.4): a *divisible* part $\Omega_e$, rents, revenues, services and the material gains of capability, and a *positional* part $\Omega_s$, decisive standing, the power to set the rules and the loss of being subordinated. The levers act on the two parts differently, and on the channels of the loop differently (Proposition S2.4).

The most direct change takes the frontier itself out of the contest by pooling effort. Where the value of capability is common to all, private incentives give too little intensity rather than too much, and the residual failure is speed (Proposition S10.1). Where winning still confers dominance on whoever is outside the pool, what pooling changes is the set of equilibria.

**Proposition 10.1 (A common frontier) [num].** In the contest below, a frontier that pools effort and confers no dominance among those who pool it makes cooperation an equilibrium beside the race while the value of winning is moderate, and the pooled effort it needs rises steeply with that value. *Of $n$ actors, cooperators hold effort $e_c$ and racers choose theirs; whoever wins the decisive contest gains $\Omega$, and every loser bears $L_b$. Under separate frontiers each effort enters the contest separately; under a common frontier the $n_c$ cooperators pool their effort into one entity of strength $(n_ce_c)^r$, whose common value $\Omega_c$ is split equally and confers no dominance among them. With $n=4$, $\Omega=\Omega_c=L_b=c=1$, a shared loss $\ell=0.3$, $e_c=0.05$ and $r=1.3$: under separate frontiers the only pure equilibrium is all racing; under a common frontier all cooperating and all racing are both strict equilibria, and cooperating is a best response iff trust in each rival exceeds about 0.61; with a smaller pooled effort, $e_c=0.03$, only all racing remains; and, with $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ fixed, the pooled effort at which racing against cooperators stops paying more than cooperating rises from 0.0054 to 0.035, 0.14 and 0.49 as $\Omega$ rises from 0.5 to 1, 2 and 4.*

*Scope.* One parameterization, with racers' intensities at the symmetric fixed point of best responses, each verified as a global best response; cases without a symmetric pure equilibrium are excluded. Where a deviator best-responds to fixed intensities, the trust needed is about 0.82; with $n=2$, all cooperating is an equilibrium for $r$ up to about 1.2. *Proof:* S10 [num: legacy v4.5, N22].

A common frontier does not end the contest. Allocation within a joint project is itself a contest, and the project, as the largest carrier, has the largest return to influence over its correctors (Proposition 4.3). Effort in the pool benefits every member alike while its cost falls on the contributor, so selection within the pool favours the member that supplies least (compare Lemma 3.11), and cooperation needs the pooled effort above a threshold that rises steeply with the value of winning; Discussion X10 therefore sets contributions by formula. When the value of winning is large, keeping ahead of those outside the pool is the race moved up a level.

**Proposition 10.2 (What sharing, pooling and convertibility change) [thm].** Sharing and provision not won in the contest can stabilize equal standing, and cutting the conversion of success into control removes the reflexive channels; none of them makes the selected intensity zero while a positional prize remains. *In the contest of Section 3.4, with prize $\Omega=\Omega_e+\Omega_s$:*
1. *Pooling payoffs, so that each actor receives $1-\beta$ of its own payoff and $\beta$ times the average of all, makes an actor holding $\theta_0\in(-1,n-1)$ act as if it held a self-model that rises strictly with $\beta$ and reaches $n-1$ at $\beta=1$. At $\theta_0=-1$, and under relative selection, pooling only scales payoff differences by $1-\beta$, which changes no choice and no ordering of carriers.*
2. *Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize, with the promise kept with probability $\varrho$, is the contest with the effective prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}=(1-\beta\varrho)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ when the shared part is divided among all $n$, the winner included, and $\Omega_{\rm eff}=\big(1-\beta\varrho\,n/(n-1)\big)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ when it goes to the $n-1$ others, the convention of Proposition 3.5(4) and Proposition 5.8. Equilibrium intensity under every common self-model, dissipation and the race hazard are proportional to $\Omega_{\rm eff}$. Under relative selection in one field with $r\le1$, the uninvadable intensity is $r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$. It is positive for every $\beta\varrho<1$ when the share is divided among all, and for every $\beta\varrho<(n-1)/n$ when it goes to the others; with $\Omega_s>0$ it is positive for every $\beta$ under the first convention, since then $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s$, and under the second iff $\Omega_s>\Omega_e/(n-1)$.*
3. *Where capacity receives provision $k_0$ not won in the contest, a smaller convertible share stabilizes equal standing (Proposition 2.3(1)); without provision the convertible share sets only the level of capacity, with or without diffusion, so winnings made non-convertible stabilize standing only together with provision. Where success cannot be converted into control over correction ($\chi=0$), the reflexive channels of Proposition 2.2 carry no gain, the return to influence over correctors is zero (Proposition 4.3), and the correction that evaluation requires falls to its value without second-order selection, $A/c_{\rm ev}$ (Proposition 4.5(1)).*

*Scope.* Parts (1) and (2) hold in the one-shot contest and its relative-selection version; part (3) collects results stated with their conditions in Sections 2.2 and 4. *Proof:* S10 [num: SH: with a positional part as large as the divisible one, sharing all of the divisible prize only halves the uninvadable intensity, from 0.5 to 0.25].

**Three levers, and where each acts.** Pooling widens the effective self of any actor that values its absolute payoff, and does nothing under relative selection: it is a lever on persons and firms, not on the selection among them. Sharing scales every channel of the loop by $1-\beta$, but only if it is credible: contemporaneous, escrowed or under shared control, before decisiveness. Sharing promised for after a decisive win is worth breaking (Proposition 5.8), so it raises the value of a decisive position instead. Changing what winning converts into acts channel by channel (Section 2.2): through capacity it needs provision not won in the contest, which lowers the won share $\omega$, since a smaller convertible share alone sets only the level; through rule support it is correction, and the cut $\chi\to0$, which removes the reflexive closure outright.

**The positional part cannot be divided.** Toward it a rival's standing is one's own subordination, $\kappa_s\le0$ in (8), and relative concern is accurate (Proposition 3.6(2)). Sharing does not reach it: however much of the divisible prize is shared among all, the uninvadable intensity stays at least $r\Omega_s/(nc)$ (part (2)) [thm]. Lowering decisiveness does not remove it, since a lottery can still award a position beyond sanction. It can only be kept from forming, by not building capability of the decisive class and by verified limits on what any winner may do, or held in common custody, so that no party can use it alone (Corollary 5.7(a)). Section 3.4 located the crux of design here: what a design must limit is what winning confers.

**What should diffuse.** Diffusion is the third inflow not tied to rank and lowers the gain in every channel (Section 2.2), so openness is a lever, not a concession. It lowers the gain without changing the game, and what diffuses is leverage as well as provision: in the author's words, open models are "a huge leverage", "both good and bad" [corpus: meta-attractor]. The line is drawn by function and by two thresholds of Table 7.1, enforcement and coverage, not by who leads. Knowledge, useful services and provision diffuse. Capability of the decisive class does not, since every actor it reaches gains a deviation that can become decisive before it is answered, and each such deviation must satisfy (14) on its own (Proposition 5.4); nor do the weights of systems in classes that admission constrains, which once released no admission reaches; nor hazardous variety beyond contestable coverage, which raises the uncovered share that survival needs to stay summable (Theorem 5.13). What does not diffuse is held in common or not built [def].

**Remark 10.3 (Concentration at two orders).** At the first order more contenders make the race fiercer (Proposition 5.1(4)); at the second, cultivated influence over correctors rises with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)), which calls for removing common control. The design that serves both merges the frontier and disperses and insulates correction, keeping several lineages inside the merged frontier against common-mode failure (Proposition 6.9). Neither "centralize" nor "decentralize" is the answer, because the two orders call for opposite forms.

### 10.3 Safeguards, verification and participation

Four safeguards make consequences return on the host of Section 3. **(I1)** A liability above the private advantage of load, enacted in advance for anticipated harms and on the alarm for others, so that effective liability exceeds the advantage once rule-making is uncaptured; for harms beyond the assets of the liable this is regulation in advance rather than liability (Shavell 1984), and near a threshold a quantity does better than a price (Weitzman 1974). **(I2)** Independent correctors in evaluation and rule-making, with $d(t)$ above $d^\ast(t)$ by a margin. **(I3)** Independent evidence: masking kept below its threshold, or a conservative alarm on a bound on masking established independently of the field (Remark 4.9). **(I4)** All three in force before the corrector domains are captured.

**Theorem 10.4 (Sufficient safeguards, their margin, and a necessary condition) [thm].** The four safeguards return consequences to their source, and keep the host above its floor throughout iff liability exceeds the private advantage by a margin that the host sets; without the margin a forgiving host dips below its floor and recovers, and a host that degrades by itself below a threshold does not. *Let selection be replicator dynamics among carriers that differ only in load $e\in[e_{\min},e_{\max}]$, with a carrier of load $e_{\min}$ present, on the host (3) with floor $V_{\min}$, and let $h_Ve_{\min}<\rho(1-V_{\min})$.*
1. *Sufficiency, with a margin. Under (I1)–(I4), effective liability exceeds the private advantage after a finite time, for unanticipated harms after an alarm that fires above the floor, and the load falls to $e_{\min}$; on the host (3) the long-run level $1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho$ exceeds the floor. From a state above the floor at which the margin $\Delta=\tau_{\rm eff}-\nu$ takes force, the trajectory stays above the floor throughout iff $\Delta\ge\Delta_{\min}$, a threshold set by the host, the floor, that state's composition and the speed of selection. Below it, the host of (3) dips under its floor and recovers. A host that regenerates less at every level and degrades by itself below a threshold $V_u$ does not recover, so there the margin must also keep the transient above $V_u$, and the floor must be set no lower than $V_u$ for crossing it to be the first loss.*
2. *Non-redundancy. For each of (I1)–(I3) there are parameters satisfying the other two under which the host crosses its floor, and parameters under which all three come into force only after capture and the host crosses it.*
3. *A necessary condition. Under (reach), with a race carrier present, a trajectory viable at every horizon keeps the cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ from tending to $+\infty$, and so has long-run average effective liability, the lim sup of its time average, at least the private advantage.*

*Scope.* The margin belongs to the transient, not to the long run. With the race strategy at 99% of activity, a margin of 0.08 from the start lets the forgiving host dip to 0.14 before recovering, and $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.108, 0.165 and 0.355 for floors of 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5; on a threshold host $\Delta_{\min}$ rises from 0.26 to 0.51 as the host's own threshold rises from 0.25 to 0.48, and a shortfall there is permanent [num: HO]. Part (3) is necessary, not sufficient: a liability whose time average tends to the advantage while the cumulative advantage grows like $\sqrt t$ lets the host cross its floor [num: HO]. The theorem computes, for one fixed strategy, an instance of the boundary of viable parameters; the correctable kernel of Definition 8.1 takes a union over strategies and an intersection over disturbances, and is not computed here. Enactment is not force: liability enacted while rule-making is captured acts as $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ (Proposition 9.2), which is what (I4) is for. *Proof:* S10.

Verification by rivals is correction too, and Section 4 applies to it. A rival's verifier is captured by none of the sources it counts if its full inbound row sum is below one, counting conversion from the verified party, from its own side and from shared sources such as models, supply chains and professional paradigms; rivalry keeps the verified party's contribution near zero and bounds nothing else in the row (Proposition S10.2). Rival verifiers also raise false alarms, can collude against third parties and check compliance rather than judgment, and an accusation between rivals can trigger prevention, so joint resolution precedes any response.

Participation has its own conditions (Proposition S10.3). Under complete information an arrangement is efficient for a pair of types iff its survival-weighted joint value exceeds the sum of their survival-weighted race payoffs, and an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff. Under private types and without verification, each type's interim share must be independent of its report, and the arrangement is feasible iff the joint value covers the sum of the types' best race payoffs; it can therefore fail where strong types are rare although cooperation is efficient for every pair [num: legacy v4.5, K7], the mutual optimism that makes bargaining fail under private information (Fearon 1995). Verification that reveals types removes the information rents, and the constraint relaxes as survival in the race falls below survival under the arrangement.

Participation benefits, voting rights and protection against unilateral domination are three different things, and a design keeps them apart. Participation can be made individually rational through shares of value and access to services; voting rights need not follow capability; and protection against domination is fixed first and not traded for participation. If, for some set of actors, participation and the independence of correction cannot both be had, the design is infeasible for them, and says so rather than buy participation with control over correction.

### 10.4 The generative loop and its closure

The reflexive structure of Section 2 has no sign of its own: what success converts into decides whether the loop consumes the shared conditions or renews them. The author's book states the two signs as two loops of the same structure [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十四章]. In the *generative loop*, security makes trust possible, trust cooperation, cooperation abundance, abundance sufficiency and slack, and these allow wider care and longer foresight, which make better order and more security (安全→信任→合作→富饶→满足与余裕→更广之仁、更远之智→更好之序与安全). In the *degenerative loop*, fear produces defence, defence struggle, struggle seizure and concentration, and these scarcity and resentment; trust collapses, and fear deepens (恐惧→防御→斗争→掠夺与集中→稀缺与不满→信任之崩→更深之恐惧). The first turns more agents into generators; the second turns more of them into defenders, instruments or exiles. In institutions the generative loop runs from shared capability to distributed agency, to plural observation, criticism, maintenance and repair, to the renewal of the commons, and so to a greater capacity for cooperation and further shared capability. It is carried by funding, education, stewardship and maintenance, shared infrastructure, independent evaluation, conflict resolution, succession, protection against enclosure, real exit and appeal, and the material security that makes restraint survivable [corpus: meta-attractor]. Goodwill is not a carrier.

The criterion is generative or degenerative, not cooperative or competitive. Cooperation on the wrong thing can be worse than competition on it: a cartel is cooperation, and inclusion that stops at a club over-produces the harm it does not bear (Proposition 3.12). Bounded competition in products, science and service continues inside a generative order; what is removed is competition for control over the shared conditions and over the correctors.

Each loop of Section 7.1, and the drive behind them, has a dual: a strand of the generative loop with a carrier, a condition and a test. The five strands are the marks G6, G2, G5, G3 and G7 of Section 8.2 stated as loops; G1 and G4 take the forms of the safeguards of Section 10.3 and of custody in Section 10.5.

| Strand | Dual of | Carrier | Condition | Test |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Reasoning instead of winning** (讲理 rather than 讲赢) [corpus: alignment-selection; jian-and-wenyan] | The naturalized narrative, and the alienated language that carries it | Derivations from public premises; verifiable evidence; protocols agreed in advance; costly signals | Objectives not opposed on the topic of talk: an effective bias below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16) | Informativeness between rivals rises as contested stakes fall |
| **Reward separated from advantage** | Compounding | Separation of reward from authority over correction; sharing as gains arise | A structural gain below one with margin, evaluated at the present distribution of standing, where the reflexive channel's gain scales with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)); $\chi\to0$; a cap on any provider's share of essential services | Conduct changes in proportion to what is shared; where it does not, advantage runs through channels outside the shared prize (C6) |
| **Provision beneath the game** | The drive: the belief in a struggle for survival, and the fear of falling behind | Shared provision, good enough, distributed and replaceable: the $k_0$ of the loop gain, acting through capacity | Not tied to standing; no inferiority in receiving it; growing with the stakes, so that the won share $\omega$ does not rise (Proposition 5.17) | The weight on relative position falls where provision is secure (C8); $1+\theta$, the ratio of conduct's response to shared harm and to private cost, rises with provision relative to the stakes (Test Γ) |
| **Correction in common hands, plural and decorrelated** | Capture of the rules, and adopt or be out; masking | Certified correctors; lineages that do not fail from one cause; audited disclosure of conversion; exit from providers | Funded by formula, appointed at arm's length, growing at the rate of Proposition 4.5, raised by $w\delta_h$ where it rests on human capacity (Proposition 4.6) | Correctors' row sums stay below one as the field grows, and their misses stay uncorrelated |
| **Pace bound to understanding** | Regulator lag | Thresholds in capability and effective compute tied to measured coverage per hazard class (E3), with stopping and rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2); drilled human capacity | $a\tau_c<1$ with margin for each hazard mode (Proposition 5.12); the uncovered share summable (Theorem 5.13) | Catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours stay level as capability grows (C3a) |

For persons, provision beneath the game removes the ground of the belief that one must defeat others first. In the corpus's words, the common foundation does not prescribe how anyone lives; it lets everyone be entitled to live without first defeating others (共同基础不是规定所有人如何生活，而是让所有人不必通过战胜别人，才有资格活着) [corpus: shengsheng, 至善 §三]. For states, service obligations and common custody act on survival itself; for providers, $k_0$ is literally capability not won: a common layer of compute, evaluation infrastructure and services. Each strand faces the questions the pathological loops answer: how it keeps obtaining resources, how it meets free riding and the pressure to expand, how it allows itself to be corrected, and how it avoids turning "protecting the whole" into a purpose no one can challenge. Exit is free from providers and services, where it is selection with the opposite sign; it is not free from the common constraints, whose protection depends on there being no outside to them (Lemma 3.3(2)).

**How the generative order persists.** In the meta-attractor of winning the loop runs through the winners: success buys support for the rule that selected it. In the generative order it cannot, because the second strand cuts that conversion, and support that no carrier can claim is a term common to all, which relative selection cancels (Lemma 3.3). The generative order is therefore not selected *among* carriers (Proposition 4.4). Inside it carriers are still selected, for usefulness; what is not selected among them is the rule itself. The rule persists as a common constraint persists: by coverage, so that no one can step outside the common floor; by financing fixed in formula, so that its correctors depend on no one's success; and by a loop that runs through the whole, in which provision, security and slack make the rule worth keeping to everyone at once. Its positive loop runs through the system, not through its winners; its negative loop gives it correctability, through correctors funded and appointed so that no one keys them. A design in which those rewarded for contribution come to hold the positions that maintain the rule takes the path of the imperial examination, created to dilute hereditary capture and turned over centuries into a selection for the examination's form: an anti-attractor can become an attractor. The corpus counts this the most serious objection to its own prescriptions, every one of which is an institutional damper [corpus: meta-attractor].

**Is the generative order an attractor?** Because it is not selected among carriers, whether it attracts is a question about the whole-level loop and about coverage, and it is a conjecture [conj]; its persistence has to be designed and measured. The same mathematics gives one caution. A cooperative order whose loop gain greatly exceeds its damping also resists the correction of its own errors, and a balancing loop that answers late overshoots (Remark S2.3). Plurality of purposes, correctors and lineages is therefore a requirement, not a decoration (Section 3.7), and it does a second job: an order whose forms can fail and be replaced can be reformed in bounded time, where one that has frozen cannot (Section 11.4).

**The reform dilemma.** Reform needs influence, and the game is what produces influence. The path by which a reformer gains the capacity to change the rules runs through the selection the reform is meant to change, so wherever that selection has closed, it removes the reform before the capacity to make it arrives: those who reach the positions from which rules are changed are those the rules favoured, and they arrive formed by them. This is the guardian problem in motion. The theory has two answers, and neither is a better guardian. Reform can act where the selection has not yet closed: at a keystone while its window is open (Section 4.5), or in a crisis with a design prepared, and its activation committed, in advance (Sections 11.4 and 12.1). And the capacity to reform can be built outside the contest for influence, by correction funded by formula and keyed to no one, so that it does not have to be won.

**Remark 10.5 (The guardian test).** Every remedy for the selection of selectors is itself a selector. Philosopher-kings, priestly classes, enlightened stewards and new universities have each been proposed as the guardians who would hold the line, and each is a corrector to which Section 4 applies; the author's question to every such proposal is how to resolve "the corruption of the constraining mechanism itself" [corpus: meta-attractor]. No better guardian is an answer. The author's book gives the mechanism and the alternative. Regulation can itself become a reinforcing loop, power producing resources and resources proving the power necessary, so it cannot be entrusted to one unconstrained final controller: it must divide its powers, publish its reasons, be reviewed on a schedule, receive independent feedback, and allow appeal and exit. The regulators must themselves be regulated (调节者亦须被调节), and real order is not finding people who are always right but building a structure in which error cannot grow without limit (真正之秩序，非得永远正确之人，乃立错误不能无限扩大之构) [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十四章]. Every arrangement, and every institution within it, is therefore asked four questions before adoption, the provision, the commons of understanding and the correctors of this section included, since each can become a position that winning reaches. *Capture:* are its correctors' full inbound row sums below one, counting every party and shared source (Proposition 4.10)? *Withdrawal:* does every closure it makes state what would end it? *Totalization:* can it make itself the whole, so that "protecting the whole" becomes a purpose no one can challenge? *Distribution of understanding:* does understanding go to everyone, or to a class whose position depends on others' not understanding? The author's answer to the last is that "everyone has access to the understanding itself directly" [corpus: alignment-selection].

### 10.5 Design by function

The design question is usually posed along ideological axes: centralization or decentralization, the individual or the collective, markets or states. The mechanisms support neither pole. Each counter-mechanism of Part I requires a particular form, and the forms differ by function: coverage requires one rule for all, independence requires many hands, and a position beyond sanction must be held in common or not formed. In the author's words, "I don't think decentralization is the fundamental issue. It is the molochlization selection process that makes [centralization or decentralization] dangerous" [corpus: meta-attractor]. The last column names the rows of Table 9.1 that each form answers.

| Function | What the mechanism requires | Form that follows | Answers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Common constraints: admission, the return of consequences, levies | Coverage of every capable actor; for every type that can evade, an expected sanction at least its gain from evasion; equal incidence, so that the rule does not re-sort the compliant (Lemma 3.3) | **One rule for all** | R1, R2, R8a, R8b |
| Custody of capability of the decisive class | No unilateral holding before a decisive position can form (Corollary 5.7(a)) | **Common custody, or not built** | R4 |
| Correction and evaluation | Independence and decorrelation: full inbound row sums below one (Proposition 4.10), growth at the required rate (Proposition 4.5), measured correlation of misses and control evaluations in which the red team knows the protocol (Proposition 6.9) | **Many hands**, certified, of different lineage, funded by formula | R3a, R3b |
| Who sets standards | One standard with many measurers makes standard-setting a keystone (Section 4.5), and Goodhart acts on it | **Standard-setters certified and rotating, separated from measurers and measured**, with held-out metrics committed in advance and revealed only at evaluation | R3b, R6 |
| Measurement and language | Shared, checkable, dynamic indicators (Remark 4.9) | **One standard, many measurers** | R6 |
| Disclosure of conversion data | Certificates are only as good as the data on conversion into correctors, and omission is the cheapest capture (Lemma 3.3(3)) | **Mandatory, audited disclosure** from every party, the drafting parties included, with a sanction above the gain from omission | R3a, R3b: the data of every certificate |
| Pace | The uncovered hazardous share summable; for each hazard mode $a\tau_c<1$ with margin (Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.12) | **Pace bound to understanding:** capability thresholds tied to measured coverage per class and input thresholds that tighten with algorithmic progress (E3), with stopping and rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2) | R5 |
| Human decision authority | Among actors who price in a certain lock, keeping AI out of the decisions it would lock is a strict equilibrium without trust (Proposition 6.4); human capacity kept above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3) | **Narrow E1 first, broad E1 as the scope** (Section 11.3), with drilled human fallback and an evidence channel independent of AI | R9 |
| Alignment and control research | Weak incarnation arises under any developer; what lowers the tax is a public good that competitors undersupply; masked divergences escape evaluation (Proposition 6.7, Lemma 3.11, Remark 6.8) | **Commonly funded, openly published research**; at admission, control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation, tied to capability thresholds | R8a, by lowering the tax and bounding masked classes |
| Learning about the design itself | Global, irreversible failures allow no second attempt; reversible ones can be learned from (Lemma 3.11) | **Bounded parallel variants above a common floor, with shared evaluation and adoption by evidence** | — |
| Provision of essential services | Good enough, distributed, replaceable; a cap on any provider's share | **Distributed provision under common guarantees** of continuity and portability | R4, through the won share (Proposition 5.17) |
| The frontier of hazardous capability | Merging lowers the race at the first order and raises the return to influence at the second; it also loses yardstick competition and lineage diversity (Remark 10.3) | **Merged accounting and custody of the frontier, plural lineages inside it, correction outside it**, contributions by formula | R4; R3a, R3b |
| Standing of those affected | Standing is owed to all who bear the consequences (V3), and inclusion that stops at a club over-produces the harm it does not bear (Proposition 3.12) | **A chamber of those affected**, drawn by a rule no participant controls, whose consent governs the slow loops | R7 |
| Due process against correctors | Every corrector is a selector that can err or be captured, and every closure states what would end it (Section 4; Remark 10.5) | **An appeal of different lineage and funding** from the correctors it reviews, with standing, access to evidence and deadlines | R3b; R7 |
| Authority (V4) | Answerable to those affected; not purchasable by success; set by row sums and contestability, **not by the degree of concentration** | **Separation rule plus chamber**: no body both revises the levy formula and appoints evaluators, and neither executes findings | R3a, R3b; R7 |

Every row of Table 9.1 has a form here, and Table S9.2 carries each row on to its threshold, mark and test. The forms are derived from function, as design principles for commons were derived from the institutions that lasted (Ostrom 1990), and with the same caution about scale: nested governance of a planetary commons is itself an open problem (Stern 2011).

**Federations as a precedent at both orders.** Competition among members for capital and residents can become a race to the bottom, the pressure of winning among members, and the strongest members can capture the centre, the selection of the selectors. At the first order, common minimum standards bind every member, and fiscal equalization gives members capacity they do not have to win, the $k_0$ of the loop gain. At the second, equal representation of small members and supermajorities for constitutional change keep the centre from being bought by the largest, a precedent for voting that does not follow compute. The same federal form can select for winning or for contribution; what decides is what it rewards (X10).

**A candidate configuration.** One configuration is developed in Discussion X10 far enough to expose its assumptions, as one candidate among those that Section 12.1 compares under one range of uncertainty. It is a set of separately held functions, not one institution. Common provision, standard-setting, evaluation, disclosure, rules and the levy formula, execution, custody, a chamber of those affected, and appeal are each held by a different body, separately financed and separately corrected, so that correction lies outside what it corrects. On a planet "outside" means outside the function: a different source of funds, a different lineage, and certificates anyone can recompute from public data. Each function is still held by one body. That is a separation of powers at one scale, monocentric function by function, and not a polycentric complex of overlapping jurisdictions that adjust to one another, which is a different candidate; nesting the functions at national, regional and planetary levels above a common floor is an option, not a requirement, since Table 9.1 constrains functions, not the number of centres. Its separation rule is the last row of the table. Supermajorities and the chamber's consent govern the slow loops, amendment and the formula, while the fast loop, restricting a specific capability, stays fast with review after the fact, since every veto point adds delay and the margin of Proposition 5.12 depends on it. The levy that funds correction is indexed to the coverage each hazard class requires, and its formula is itself a prize (X10). Providers have voice without vote, with their own conversion disclosed: a voice in rule-making keeps the local knowledge and compliance it brings (Ostrom 1990), a vote would let success weigh in on its own assessment (Proposition 4.3), and the tension between the two is real. The configuration states its end: if coverage falls below its participation threshold, or a stated number of its certificates fail for a stated period for reasons not attributable to a party under assessment, it dissolves into its separately held functions, while protections against unilateral domination stay entrenched. By Proposition 4.3 such an arrangement is the largest conversion target the theory describes, so capture of its own correctors (D4) is its hardest case, not a design row. Where execution through a host jurisdiction fails against the strongest participant (D5), what remains is the coercive restriction of frontier inputs, which the design does not settle: it has one home, the compared-not-settled tier of Section 12.1, under three conditions of its own, and the theory does not derive that its net effect is positive.

Section 11 takes from here the five strands, the forms and their stated ends, and asks whether such an order can be reached from where the trajectory stands: survivable at every stage, preferred by each participant at each step, and held long enough not to be re-absorbed, with trust an outcome of the transition rather than its precondition.

# Part IV. Transition and judgment

Part IV's results on trust, signals, pauses and dwell hold in the models they state; its tracks, readiness dimensions and requirements of a reachable path are definitions, each row with the support it cites; its precedents are observations whose limiting conditions are part of what they show; and its judgment is a recommendation under the standard of Section 8.1.

## 11. A reachable transition

> **Claim.** An end state that every participant would keep is not thereby one they can reach [def]. A path is reachable when it is survivable at every stage, preferred by every participant at every step given its beliefs, and held at every step until the slow variable that step moves has crossed its threshold [def]. In the field model, a step that moves a parameter without carrying the state across its threshold, or that is released on share levels before its dwell time, is re-absorbed, so graduated steps reach only as a ratchet; the hold a reform needs grows without bound with the age of capture, unless a lineage of the alternative persists that can be recruited when the rule changes [thm]. Narrow E1 among actors who price in a certain lock, and a costly signal that separates a survival-first actor from a relative one, need no trust; where the first steps are of this kind, trust is an outcome of the transition, not its precondition [thm]. A pause that changes nothing buys time, not survival [thm].
>
> **Why.** Trust is needed to cooperate against a possible defector, to be believed and to stay, and a lower value of winning relaxes all three at once [thm]. Rule support falls only at the rate at which it is corrected, while shares lag it, so levels improve before safety does [thm]; and a prediction carried only by assurances meets the third erosion [thm]. Each commitment moves a slow variable with its own time constant and meets its own compensating loop, so the commitments run as parallel tracks, the slowest started first [def].

### 11.1 Trust, and what relaxes it

Trust enters a transition in three places. To cooperate against a possible defector, an actor needs its belief $\varpi$ that the other will cooperate to reach the threshold $g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ of Proposition 8.6; where the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating, $T>R$, verification has to supply what belief does not. To be believed, a sender needs the receiver not to discount what it says for what it gains by saying it, which relative accounting erodes (Proposition 5.16). To stay, a participant needs confidence that the arrangement will persist, which the net gain from exit erodes where that gain grows with the capability held in check (Proposition S8.5). Confidence in reliable instruments of verification lets parties cooperate without good will, but installing them is itself a first step taken under $\varpi$.

**Corollary 11.1 (What relaxes the trust constraints) [thm].** Whatever lowers the value of winning relative to the joint value relaxes all three constraints at once; verification relaxes the first only on what it observes; a sanction relaxes the first and the third only while it can still be imposed. *In the notation of Proposition 8.6, Proposition 5.16 and Proposition S8.5, with $g_C,g_D>0$:*
1. *Raising the value of mutual restraint relative to the value of winning, so that $T-R$ falls while $R>P-F$, raises $g_C$ and lowers both the belief threshold and the timely detection that cooperation needs, $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$ (Theorem 8.7(1)); a wider working objective, a larger $\theta$, lowers the effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$ of talk; and a smaller relative term in every payoff lowers the net gain from exit, $V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$.*
2. *Where $T-P+F>0$, a larger probability $\upsilon$ of timely detection raises $g_C$ and, where $P>S$, lowers $g_D$, so it lowers the belief threshold; it carries no information about what it does not observe.*
3. *A larger sanction $F$ raises $g_C$, and it deters an irreversible, public exit while $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$. It cannot be imposed on a defector whose position has become decisive, so against such a deviation it acts only through the bound $\bar p_d$ on the chance of breakout (Proposition 5.4(2)).*
4. *Lowering the vulnerability of restraint, $P-S$, lowers $g_D$ and with it the belief threshold.*

*Proof:* S11.

Sharing the divisible stakes and provision beneath the game lower $T-R$ and the relative term of exit; where they also lower the price of standing, they widen working objectives (Proposition 3.9) and with them the range of informative talk [thm∣prem]. A common frontier lowers $P-S$ by pooling the restrainers' effort (Proposition 10.1) [num]. Sanctions relax assurance and exit at the price of escalation, which the comparison counts in $\Theta$ (Section 8.1). Trust is mainly an outcome of the transition, not its precondition. It follows from conduct that a lower value of winning makes rational and verification makes visible; the one step it must precede is the installing of instruments, and the first steps of Section 11.2 need none. In the author's words, "Trust is not a prerequisite—it is an outcome. It arises only when we have a shared understanding of reality and can reasonably expect others to act accordingly" [corpus: selfishness-spiral, 2025-03-14].

Not every obstacle is a lack of trust, and the remedy follows the obstacle. A misjudged shared loss calls for evidence; a real conflict of incentives, for changed payoffs and enforcement; a coordination failure, for verification and graduated reciprocation (Osgood 1962); commitment and capture over time, for independent correction and steps each held for its dwell. Graduated steps answer the third only: they do not move a conflict of incentives, and against capture they reach only as a ratchet (Section 11.4). Narrow E1 is a first step that needs no trust. It removes AI from decisions irreversible within the time for deliberation, not from the detection that answers a breakout, so it leaves the chance that a rival's breakout becomes decisive, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, where it was (Remark 5.5); and among actors who price in a certain lock it is a gain, not a cost (Proposition 6.4(1)) [thm].

### 11.2 Prediction, evidence and signals

A prediction about a game whose outcome turns on what the players believe is one of the game's inputs, and it acts through two beliefs. Where an actor misjudges the shared loss, a derivation it can check corrects its own accounting: an actor that holds the prediction, and finds that restraint gives it higher survival whether its rival restrains or races, restrains whatever it believes about the others (Proposition S8.1(2)) [thm]. Where racing persists because each expects restraint to be exploited while a cooperative equilibrium exists (Section 5.4), the same derivation moves $\varpi$, and the equilibrium with it. That is counter-performativity: a model of behaviour can make the world less like itself (MacKenzie 2006) [obs], and it is the sense in which this paper's prediction is written to be falsified by being shared (Section 0). It acts only where the outcome turns on a belief the prediction can change: it does not change what the game selects whatever is believed, and where racing is dominant in a one-shot game the belief it would correct is simply correct.

Messages about a shared hazard are not credible merely because the hazard is shared, since a sender gains from the receiver's restraint whatever it intends (Proposition 5.16(3)); a prediction carried only by assurances meets the third erosion. It has to be carried by what a receiver can check without trusting the sender: a derivation from public premises; verifiable evidence, such as third-party disclosure of incidents and joint evaluation suites; protocols agreed in advance, which commit a sender's disclosures before it knows what they will show (Kamenica and Gentzkow 2011); demonstrations that make a dynamic visible; and costly signals [def]. Shared understanding removes one road to conflict, since under common priors mutual optimism alone cannot produce war (Fey and Ramsay 2007) [thm]. It does not remove the others: in an idealized experiment, falling behind drove unsafe choices while the stated level of shared risk changed them little, a finding of exploratory analysis (EV11) [obs]. A change of prediction is the first step, not a sufficient one. The next is conduct a rival can read, which is also what tips a professed doctrine that few hold in private (Section 5.4); and the payoffs that make falling behind unsurvivable have to change as well, which is what the arrangements below and Section 12 are for.

**Proposition 11.2 (When keeping AI out of one's own decision loops is a signal) [thm].** A step that the lock makes cheap for a survival-first actor and the contest makes costly for a relative one separates them; a step that is cheap to imitate buys restraint only in proportion to what it costs a relative actor. *A sender is survival-first and prices in the lock (type $\mathsf S$, with probability $1-\mu$) or holds a relative objective (type $\mathsf R$, with probability $\mu$). It can take a step $\mathsf E$: a verifiable, durable removal of AI from its own strategic decision loops for a period, open to inspection, which costs type $\mathsf t$ the amount $c_{\mathsf t}$. A receiver restrains iff its belief that the sender is $\mathsf S$ is at least $\varpi^\ast\in(0,1)$; its restraint is worth $\Pi_{\mathsf t}>0$ to type $\mathsf t$, including, for $\mathsf R$, what $\mathsf R$ gains by exploiting it.*
1. *An equilibrium in which $\mathsf S$ takes the step, $\mathsf R$ does not, and the receiver restrains after the step and not otherwise exists iff $c_{\mathsf S}\le\Pi_{\mathsf S}$ and $c_{\mathsf R}\ge\Pi_{\mathsf R}$.*
2. *If $0\le c_{\mathsf R}<\Pi_{\mathsf R}$, there is no such equilibrium.
